M
Global Insight
2027 Global Economics Outlook
Powered  by AI, Tested by Oil
Global growth remains solid, but inflation has been persistently high, and central banks in developed 
markets are beginning a modest hiking cycle. Since our Mid-year Outlook, growth has modestly 
outperformed expectations, but the inflation path has become murky. Even central banks that are not 
hiking are keeping policy tighter than might otherwise have been expected. Our view of a brief tightening 
cycle relies on energy prices continuing to fall. The key drivers of economic growth in the US are the AI-
driven capex build-out and consumer spending; the latter fueled by wealth and full employment. US 
growth is helping insulate the rest of the world from downside risks. We rely on alternative scenarios to 
illustrate the potential paths for the global economy through 2028.
Morgan Stanley does and seeks to do business with companies covered in Morgan Stanley Research. As a result, investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of 
interest that could affect the objectivity of Morgan Stanley Research. Investors should consider Morgan Stanley Research as only a single factor in making their investment 
decision.
For analyst certification and other important disclosures, refer to the Disclosure Section, located at the end of this report.

communications with a subject company, public appearances and trading securities held by a research analyst account. 
November 15, 2026 06:00 PM GMT
November 15, 2026 06:00 PM GMT
 
 

M
Global Insight
2
• Global growth is solid, with notable strength in the US,  moderate but 
sustained growth in Europe, and a gradually fading  deflationary cycle in 
China keeping growth there modest. In the US,  AI-driven capex and high-
income consumers are the key. In Europe,  German fiscal impetus and growth in 
the periphery are generating  momentum. The capex cycle is a global 
phenomenon, but across Asia,  the capex cycle is broadening and becoming self-
sustaining. 
• Inflation has proven persistent, as the energy price shock has  reignited 
headline inflation and raised the prospect of  second-round inflation across 
the globe. While high energy costs  will dampen growth in select economies, we 
see those headwinds  receding by year-end 2027. One theme is that the energy 
shock  creates growth-inflation dilemmas for central banks, especially in  
emerging markets. Consequently, we see global growth modestly  softening in 
2027 before recovering in 2028 to the 2026 pace. 
• Energy remains volatile, and we see growing risks of nonlinear  inflationary 
effects. Energy, food, and supply-chain pressures risk  boosting core inflation 
along with headline. In current  circumstances, the price of crude oil on screens 
is no longer a  sufficient statistic; the historically-wide spread to refined  
products and geographical dispersion of delivered prices make for a  
heterogeneous outcome across the globe. 
• Generally speaking, monetary policy is anticipating the  inflation pressure by 
tightening policy into the new year. The  risks to inflation are skewed to the 
upside, and the risks to  inflation crimping growth are rising. We revisit this issue 
in a  downside scenario. 
• Capex on AI continues to be one key driver of growth. Two of our  alternative 
scenarios explore how AI may affect the macro outcomes.  In one case, more 
aggressive adoption leads to even greater  near-term productivity gains, 
enabling stronger growth than the  baseline and yet moderating inflation. 
Conversely, if AI-driven  investment were to fall short, the drag on growth 
would be  pronounced, though not to recessionary levels. 
• As a base case, we expect gradual disinflation in developed  markets, with 
core inflation near central bank targets by 4Q28. The  speed of disinflation 
will drive market pricing of central banks.  Our forecasts are for developed 
market policy rates to peak in 1Q27  (Fed at 4.25-4.5%; ECB at 3.0% and BoJ at 
1.75%), and then as  inflation ebbs, in most economies, we project a modest 
cutting  cycle in 2028. 
• In emerging markets, the inflationary pressures are stronger, so  policy must 
lean harder in the other direction; growth softens  more, before an eventual 
reversal. China is the main exception,  where growth remains export driven and 
inflation soft. 

M
Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
3
Energy and commodity market drive monetary policy choices  in the short run, 
while the pace of AI investment and adoption will  define growth through 2028. In 
our baseline, global growth remains  relatively stable, supported by continued US 
demand, even as higher  energy costs and restrictive policy weigh on activity.  
Growth across the G4 economies edges higher across the forecast  period, despite a 
modest softening at the turn of the year from  energy costs. Emerging markets face a 
greater slowdown from the  combination of energy prices and tightening financial 
conditions. As  energy prices decline, growth recovers, supported by the continued  US 
AI boom. Across emerging markets, this spending provides a key  buoy, especially for 
the key exporters, and helps to offset the  adverse shocks from energy.

While higher energy prices continue to push headline  inflation higher, the extent 
and persistence of pass-through to  core inflation differ across economies.  We 
expect global disinflation to proceed only gradually in 2027,  becoming more firmly 
established in 2028. In Europe, higher energy  costs pass through to wages and 
services, supporting underlying  inflation even after headline inflation peaks. In the US, 
resilient  demand and a gradual improvement in productivity should allow  inflation to 
moderate, but not quickly enough to prevent additional  monetary tightening. Against 
this backdrop, the convergence toward  neutral policy rates that we previously 
envisaged has been pushed  further into 2028.  
Economic growth in the US is supported by AI-driven capital  expenditure and 
resilient household consumption, particularly among  higher-income consumers.  We 
expect growth to stay robust across the horizon, with AI-related  investment remaining 
a key driver of activity. We see the labor  market staying in a "slow-hire/slow-fire" 
equilibrium, with the  unemployment rate rising modestly but staying below 4.5%.  

M
Global Insight
4
Slow but steady employment growth  should keep consumption growth near trend. 
We forecast productivity  growth to rise by around 20bp each year over 2026-28. That  
productivity growth allows for stronger economic growth, but we do  not see a notable 
change in our inflation forecast in the baseline;  instead, we explore that mechanism in 
our alternative scenarios.  Disinflation in the forecast comes more from the ebbing 
effects of  energy and tariffs.  
AI capex is critical for both US growth and global growth  through exports; our 
equity analysts have not lowered their  baseline numbers  despite recent uncertainty 
following the US mid-term elections. The  adjustment to Morgan Stanley’s AI capex 
projections was to shift 5GW  from 2028 to 2029. This shift likely smooths supply-
demand  rebalancing. For GDP, the timing is further complicated by the  allocation of 
investment. The implications for GDP growth in the US  and the world are subtle and 
complicated. The distinction between  real versus nominal is important – pricing on 
chips and other  related goods has gone up notably, implying that the parsing of  
nominal versus real GDP growth is challenging. The type of  investment has different 
implications for trade. The pre-building  and construction aspects of data centers are 
heavily oriented to  domestic spending. For the US, however, many of the chips and 
related  computer and communications equipment are imported, which drives  exports 
elsewhere.  
In our baseline, the Federal Reserve pushes interest rates  to 4.25-4.5% by 1Q27 and 
holds steady before cutting rates in 2028  when inflation nears target late in the 
forecast period  . Markets are grappling with the murky outlook for inflation and  will 
continue to reprice expectations for policy tightening. We  think most central banks are 
wary of overtightening, and so a few  months in a row of receding inflation may be 
sufficient to stay  their hands from additional hiking after inflation peaks in 1Q27. In  the 
US, the path will likely be particularly opaque given the  historical residual seasonality 
that pushes up inflation in 1Q. But  we do expect inflation to trend down over the 
course of next year,  keeping the Fed on hold after March. Inflation makes a more  
concerted move lower, reaching just above target by 4Q28; we see the  Fed cutting 
rates by 2Q28 as the inflation path to target becomes  clear.  
In the rest of the world, the inflation and growth outlook  remains particularly 
sensitive to energy prices, although the  transmission varies considerably across 
regions.  Europe faces the strongest inflation persistence, with headline  inflation 
peaking above 4% in early 2027 in our baseline, and core  inflation remaining above 
target until mid-2028. Nevertheless, we  see exports and public spending supporting 
growth before private  demand strengthens in 2028. In Japan, higher energy prices and  
tighter financial conditions are likely to constrain consumption and  residential 
investment, although fiscal measures cushion household  incomes and structural 
investment demand remains resilient. We  expect inflation to fall below 2% from the 
second half of 2027 as  the energy impulse fades. China remains relatively insulated 
from  imported inflation, but weak household demand, the property-sector  adjustment, 
and fiscal consolidation constrain growth. We see export  strength continuing to offset 
domestic weakness, while underlying  disinflation limits nominal growth and the scope 
for a broader  recovery.

Monetary policy peaks in 1Q27 before easing in 2028 as  inflation pressures recede.  
The cycle is viewed as a modest adjustment, with a particular focus  on the persistence 
of inflation in the current environment where  there is a heightened risk of non-core 
inflation moving to core. As  noted, we expect the Fed to raise rates in December and 
March,  taking them to 4.25-4.5% by 1Q27 and then remaining on hold through  2027 
before easing in 2028. We think the ECB will hike in December  2026 and March 2027, 
taking rates to 3%, and then maintain a  restrictive stance until June 2028, when 
improving disinflation  should allow quarterly cuts to 2.25% by year-end. We see the 
BoE  raising rates in December 2026 and February 2027 and maintaining a restrictive 
stance through 2027 before  easing begins in 2Q28. The BoJ will also raise rates twice, 
we  believe, reaching 1.75% in March 2027, before pausing through 2028.  China's policy 
rates will likely remain unchanged as policymakers  rely on targeted credit and liquidity 
support rather than  broad-based easing.  
Across emerging markets, the picture remains mixed.  We see India's strong domestic 
growth and elevated food inflation  prompting the RBI to raise rates to 6.25% by April 
2027, before  easing in 2028. Brazil continues its easing cycle only in 2028 as  inflation 
expectations and fiscal uncertainty constrain policy  flexibility in the near term. Mexico 
eases to 6% by early 2027  before remaining on hold as persistent services inflation 
limits  further reductions. Growth in Mexico recovers as USMCA-related  uncertainty 
fades, while Brazil's recovery is delayed until 2028 by  restrictive financial conditions 
and weaker household demand.

We model four alternative scenarios: an AI Productivity  upside, an Aggregate 
Demand upside, an AI Investment downside, and  a Rates and Oil downside.  These 

M
Global Insight
6
scenarios highlight key risks and market debates.  
We have assumed that AI adoption gradually increases productivity  across the 
economy. In the  AI Productivity upside  scenario, the productivity gains are larger and 
come sooner. The US  sees the biggest gains, but we assume other economies also see  
gains. Growth in the US is boosted and labor demand increases,  leaving the labor 
market broadly stable, but disinflation  nevertheless continues. The Fed allows the 
faster growth without  tightening policy further, but the strong growth also prevents  
easing. Productivity improvements also benefit Europe and Japan,  although the effects 
vary across economies and materialize  gradually. China experiences stronger real 
growth but limited  nominal improvement as productivity gains reinforce disinflation.  
The current strong growth forces consideration of an  Aggregate Demand upside  
scenario, in which US investment and consumption strengthen sharply  from current 
levels, with the improvement broadening across income  groups. The unemployment 
rate falls to 3.5%, while inflation stays  just over 3% as stronger demand generates 
renewed overheating. The  Fed responds by hiking to 5.625%, just above its 2023 peak. 
Stronger US and  global demand lifts exports and investment elsewhere, particularly  in 
Europe and export-oriented Asian economies, but also generates  additional inflation 
pressures and requires more restrictive  monetary policy. Unlike the productivity 
upside, stronger growth  comes at the cost of materially higher interest rates.  
The energy price shocks have driven central banks to tighten policy.  If conditions revert 
in the Gulf, we could imagine a  Rates and Oil downside  scenario. We sketch a situation 
where oil prices rise to  $125-150/bbl and central banks tighten further to contain 
inflation,  compounding the hit to real incomes and investment. The Fed raises  rates to 
5.375%, contributing to a mild US recession in late 2027,  while European activity 
contracts as higher energy costs and  interest rates squeeze demand. Inflation 
continues to rise until  enough slack opens up in the economy. As a result, central bank  
easing is delayed until  2028.  
The criticality of AI-driven capex has led to a market debate about  a possible AI-
spending slowdown. Our  AI Investment downside  scenario, where spending plans fall 
short of expectations, sees a  correction in both economic activity and asset prices. 
Weaker  investment and negative wealth effects reduce US growth, with  
unemployment rising to 5.1% and the Fed cutting rates aggressively  to 1.375%. The 
slowdown spills over to Europe and Asia through  weaker technology demand and 
exports, while lower inflation creates  room for monetary easing.

Morgan Stanley Research
13
AI Spending and GDP Accounting
Deriving the contribution of AI spending on GDP is a complicated process, 
combining estimates of spending with the composition of the spending, and 
whether the goods and services purchased are imported or not. Further, 
distinguishing between price and volume is complicated by a lack of revenue 
transparency and lags in volume measures of goods. Despite those caveats, in the 
current AI wave, there are advantages to using microdata because the spend is 
concentrated in a small number of companies, notably hyperscalers. As a result, 
we begin with our equity analysts' capex forecasts as a starting point to map AI 
spending and its contribution to economic growth – gross domestic product.
AI investment is forecast by MS thematic and equity analysts to rise to $1.6tn 
in 2027 and $1.8tn in 2028, though there are risks that some of the 2028 
spend may be pushed into 2029. A high level of spending supports activity, but 
GDP growth depends on the change in that spending from one period to the next. 
The pace of investment growth therefore matters as much as the size of the 
announced commitments. Our baseline numbers show continued strong spending, 
but the growth rate will fall as the spending settles into a high level.
Exhibit 20:
Hyperscaler capex forecasts rise to $1.6tn in 2027 and $1.8tn in 2028, although 
the annual growth rate of nominal hyperscaler capex slows sharply from 2027 to 2028

Source: Total capex forecasts include GOOGL, AMZN, META, SPCX, ORCL, CRWV, AMD, and NBIS. Source: Morgan Stanley Research
We use two measures for GDP. The broader measure captures national-accounts 
categories that include AI spending alongside other activity; we call it AI-related 
spending. The narrower measure attempts to isolate spending specifically on AI. 
Keeping those definitions separate is important to differentiate how the build-out 
is being realized in the economy.
The allocation of that spending matters too. GDP measures domestic 
production. Imported equipment is recorded in investment and offset in 
imports, so it does not contribute to US GDP in the same way as domestic 
construction. For example, in 3Q26, AI investment contributed 1.6pp to growth 
before accounting for imports and 0.9pp after that adjustment. The difference 
shows why headline capex figures alone can overstate the direct contribution to 
US output and the important role imports play in data center construction.
To borrow from our US economics outlook, infrastructure spending has been and 
will likely continue to be the main driver of AI investment's contribution to real 
GDP growth through 2027. However, we expect a shift in composition from 2028 
if the infrastructure build-out has not been significantly delayed: although 
hyperscaler spending in 2028 will remain very significant, the increase relative to 

M
Global Insight
14
its 2027 level will be small, leading to a deceleration in the contribution from 
infrastructure spending after netting out imports. As more compute capacity 
comes online by the end of 2027, we expect adoption of AI by the average US firm 
to pick up from 2028 onward. The acceleration in software investment should 
offset some of the slowdown in infrastructure investment's contribution to growth 
and become the new driver of AI investment, as illustrated in Exhibit 21 .

AI-Related Spending vs AI-Only Spending: Contribution to Real GDP Growth, pp
Investment

Source: Bureau of Economic Analysis, Morgan Stanley forecasts (E). Note: "Related" means contribution of AI-related spending's contribution to GDP growth, 
net of imports. "AI" is our estimate of AI-only spending's contribution to GDP growth, net of imports. We focus on annual averages to reflect broader trends 
and avoid volatility in quarterly GDP statistics. 
The above forecasts help frame the global implications. Continued momentum in 
exports from Korea and Taiwan provide an early affirmation that AI-related 
imports will continue apace into 2027. We are also seeing a broadening of 
imports to include networking equipment alongside chips and computing goods 
in the trade data, which suggests the coming years will see a broader array of 
trading partners for US AI demand. For example, Mexico is important for server 
assembly, Southeast Asia is gaining exposure in networking and optical equipment, 
and Japan and Europe are increasingly involved in semiconductor equipment, 
turbines, transformers, and other power infrastructure. Alternatively, there are 
scenarios where our strategists could envision data center demand spilling over to 
Asia and EMEA. The same global investment cycle can thus support US 
construction and a wider range of exporters, with the timing and location of 
spending determined by evolving capacity constraints.

M
Global Insight
16
Growth
In our baseline forecast, we are broadly constructive on global growth through the 
end of 2028. The AI infrastructure build-out, particularly in the US, is a key driver. 
That US growth helps support growth globally, while Asia's broader capex super-cycle 
continues and, in Europe, we see sustained solid growth in the periphery alongside further 
fiscal impetus from Germany. That said, outside the US, we pencil in a brief soft patch 
around the turn of the year as energy costs bite – most visibly in Japan and the UK – 
followed by a gradual firming through 2027. For emerging markets, we expect a slowing 
over the forecast period because of high energy costs and tight monetary policy. In North 
Asia, growth slows because the torrid pace of expansion from tech exports normalizes, 
albeit at a very high level. 
Our scenarios bracket the growth risks around that baseline path. The Aggregate Demand 
upside sees faster growth and higher inflation, with interest rates accordingly much higher 
than the baseline. The AI Productivity upside has faster growth but moderate inflation, 
with interest rates rising modestly. In terms of slower growth, the AI Investment downside 
scenario assumes a drag on growth both directly through lower spending and indirectly 
through weaker asset prices weighing on consumption. The Rates and Oil downside is a 
stagflationary mix, where sharply higher oil prices boost inflation and weigh on growth, 
and central bank policy tightening adds to the drag, generating a mild recession in the US. 
We discuss each in the Alternative Scenarios section.
In our baseline, investment spending – mostly for AI, but also broadening out – and 
consumption spending keep US economic growth solid despite high energy prices and 
tightening monetary policy. Full employment and wealth ensure consumption growth 
remains solid. In our baseline, we expect productivity growth to keep rising as AI moves 
from the lab into the workplace, but only gradually – enough to help at the margin, 
though not enough to solve the inflation problem on its own. 
Exhibit 23:
The US expansion strengthens further in 2028, with capex and 
consumption both pulling their weight

For the euro area, we 
expect continued 
solid growth by 
European standards.

household spending and business investment. In 2028, we see the impulse from public 
spending fading as spending stabilizes at an elevated level. Exports have also surprised to 
the upside, helped in part by demand for electrical equipment and machinery tied to the AI 
build-out in the US. By 2028, after the squeeze from energy and tighter monetary policy 
eases, we expect consumers and domestic-facing firms to regain their footing.

In Japan, our baseline is that growth slows temporarily, because of 
the energy shock, though subsidies, domestic refining and high crude 
inventories blunt the direct hit somewhat. Energy costs and the 
accumulated weight of BoJ hikes fall squarely on household spending 
and housing investment, and activity comes close to a standstill 
around the turn of the year. By contrast, business investment should 
hold up well: with the workforce shrinking and much of the capital 
stock overdue for replacement, companies have little choice but to 
keep spending on automation and new equipment. AI build-out in the 
US adds further support, albeit indirectly: chipmakers in Asia, which 
supply the chips for US data centers, are expanding capacity with 
Japanese capital goods. As the energy shock fades, real incomes 
should recover, and the reduced consumption tax on food from April 
2027 will add further support to household purchasing power, paving the way for a 
gradual recovery in consumption.

In emerging markets, 
the slowdown lasts

developed world, 
stretching into 2028.

at an unsustainable 
pace. AI-related

bust; output stays at a high level and is supported by a broadening domestic capex cycle 
across Asia. Still, it accounts for a good part of the emerging market slowdown we see in 
2028. That is not the whole story, however: higher energy bills and higher policy rates are 
weighing on domestic spending, most visibly in economies with less fiscal room to cushion 
the blow.
India remains a bright spot. Strong balance sheets across companies and banks, together 
with ample policy buffers, should carry the economy through a weaker monsoon and the 
pressure from food prices.
Latin America is the region where the outlook improves meaningfully in 2028. Brazil 
will see a trough in growth in 2027, we believe: households are stretched by debt 
payments, inflation is eroding real wages, and the fiscal tailwind is fading. Once interest 
rates come down and credit loosens, though, spending and investment should lead a

country where we see little sign of the kind of productivity breakthrough that would 
change a country's trend growth.

Global Insight
20
AI Productivity and Labor Markets
We are beginning to see initial empirical evidence in the macro data of 
productivity gains related to AI adoption. In the US, much of the improvement so 
far reflects capital deepening as firms build compute capacity and the 
infrastructure needed to use AI. That investment is boosting output per worker. A 
sustained acceleration in total factor productivity – that is, output per worker 
adjusting for the capital stocks – would have larger implications for potential 
growth and the neutral rate. Total factor productivity (TFP) reflects true 
innovation, and that type of productivity growth helps to define the limits on 
growth that the economy can sustain without generating inflation.
However, the US productivity upswing also predates generative AI. Our 
estimates show that trend labor productivity growth rose from an annualized rate 
of about 1.6% in 2019 to 1.9% by late 2022; it has since reached 2.1% using data 
through 3Q26. Our baseline assumes a further rise to 2.3% by 2028. Some of the 
acceleration therefore reflects earlier efficiency gains. More recently, however, the 
improvement has become concentrated in industries with greater AI exposure: 
those industries contributed 2.0 percentage points of the 2.4% increase in output 
per employee over the four quarters through 2Q26.
The decomposition still points mainly to capital deepening and greater 
utilization. The broad pick-up in total factor productivity that accompanied the 
late-1990s technology boom has yet to emerge. That sequence is understandable. 
Companies first need to install capacity and integrate AI into production before 
wider efficiency gains become visible. Capital deepening supports productivity 
while investment is strong; sustained TFP growth would make the improvement 
more durable as that investment cycle matures.
We can also think of the potential growth rate of the economy in terms of trend 
productivity growth and growth in the labor supply In the US right now, those 
forces are moving in opposite directions. Trend hours growth has slowed to around 
0.3% annualized, from 0.7% before the Covid pandemic and 1.3% in 2022-23, while 
trend productivity has accelerated. The result is that our estimate of potential GDP 
growth, ~2.2% in 2026, is only slightly above the 2010-19 average of 1.9%. For 
much of the developed world, a similar pattern is relevant.
Exhibit 30:
In the US, faster productivity growth has been largely offset by slower growth in 
trend hours, leaving potential growth only modestly above pre-pandemic averages. In our 
industry decomposition of productivity, the industries with high AI exposure have accounted for 
the acceleration, and are contributing an increasing share

For the labor market, AI-related displacement looks more micro than macro.

Morgan Stanley Research
21
Employment has edged down a bit in some highly AI-exposed industries, including 
data processing and software, and roughly half of the high-AI industry sample has 
recorded some employment decline. The broader labor market has absorbed those 
losses, however, and the unemployment rate has been more or less unchanged for 
over a year. We estimate AI has raised the aggregate unemployment rate by no 
more than 15bp through 1H26, with the impact concentrated among younger 
workers in more exposed occupations. In our view, the speed of technological 
diffusion will be key; a moderate speed of diffusion that is still twice as fast as the 
internet era is likely to lead to manageable labor market disruptions, once wealth 
effects and new job creation effects are accounted for.

evidence for younger workers continues to be clearer, with a sharper rise in the UR

European evidence also suggests that productivity gains can outweigh 
employment losses. In our most conservative EU specification, each additional 
percentage point of AI adoption is associated with 12bp higher productivity growth 
and 6bp lower employment growth, alongside an estimated net GDP growth 
benefit of about 7bp. The productivity result is more robust; the employment 
effect is statistically insignificant in most specifications. In most other regions, our 
economists build little direct productivity benefit from AI into their baseline 
forecasts. The next question is how widely the gains will spread, and how quickly 
workers will move into new activities as adoption increases.
More generally, a sustained productivity acceleration would likely also raise 
the real neutral interest rate, or r*. Higher expected returns on capital would 
strengthen desired investment, increasing the rate consistent with balanced 
demand and supply. Higher productivity can boost both growth (and therefore 
earnings and equities) and interest rates, changing some of the typical correlations 
markets expect.

M
Global Insight
22
Inflation
We broadly expect inflation to peak around the turn of the year and then come down 
steadily. Headline inflation is currently elevated because oil prices have been high in 
recent months. As they move lower, headline inflation should also come down, though 
somewhat complicated by constraints on refined petroleum products around the world, 
which vary by economy. Our commodity strategists expect diesel prices to remain tight 
until mid-2027. Core inflation therefore faces headwinds from supply chains that are 
having to react to the elevated price pressures. We envisage producer price pressures 
increasing in certain sectors (e.g., chemicals, rubbers, and plastics) because buffers from 
inventories, hedges, fixed-price contracts and fiscal support have been used up. Europe, 
which imports much of its diesel, feels this most; Asia, with the largest refining buffer, 
feels it least. Only by late 2027 and into 2028, once energy and tariff price pressures are 
fully past, do we see core inflation in all the G4 economies move closer to target. 
Exhibit 32:
The oil shock is likely to stall disinflation globally, but the extent of uplift is highly 
heterogeneous –  developed markets have greater pass-through, while emerging markets tend to smooth 
prices to consumers

In the US, inflation will come down, though not as quickly, in our view, as the Fed would 
like. As the effects of energy and tariffs fade, core inflation should start trending lower in 
2027 and approach target only late in 2028. Resilient demand is likely to keep the descent 
gradual, and the modest pick-up in productivity we foresee is too small to change the 
picture, at least for now. The path for inflation, in other words, depends far more on how 
quickly the energy and tariff shocks unwind than on anything happening on the supply 
side. That combination is why we believe the Fed feels compelled to lean against second-
round effects even with a labor market that shows few signs of overheating.

Europe is even more exposed to cost-push inflation. Energy costs reach consumers quickly 
there, and we think the spillover into food and core prices, though still limited, will gather 
pace over the coming months. In the euro area, we see headline inflation peaking early in 
2027 and staying uncomfortably high through the summer before energy base effects 
start pulling it down, dipping below target in 2028. Core inflation is likely to be more 
persistent. Once higher costs feed into wages and services, they take time to work their 
way out, and we see core inflation settling at target only from mid-2028. Across the 
Channel in the UK, inflation proves sticky through 2027 before converging toward target 
in 2028.
Japan sits at the other end of the spectrum. Government subsidies to fuel wholesalers 
and electricity and gas retailers help contain gasoline prices and lower electricity and gas 
bills, limiting the pass-through of higher energy costs to consumer prices. Together with 
stable rice prices, these factors all help to keep inflation expectations anchored around 
2%. In our baseline, headline inflation slips below 2% from the second half of 2027 as the 
shock fades. The cut in the consumption tax on food in April 2027 will also pull headline 
inflation lower mechanically, though only temporarily: once the effect drops out in the 
spring of 2028, we think headline inflation will move back up toward 1.5%. Wage growth 
in a tight labor market continues to support underlying inflation, so we see prices settling 
gradually rather than sliding back toward deflation. Our view that underlying inflation 
does not reach or exceed 2% on a sustained basis is a key part of our forecast.
China, as has so often been the case, is the exception. Higher energy costs have lifted 
producer prices this year without generating much consumer inflation, and as that impulse 
fades, we think the familiar disinflationary forces will return: excess capacity, weak wage 
growth and soft household demand, now compounded by fiscal tightening.

In emerging markets, we believe the inflation episode will be more forceful and last 
longer. We see India's headline inflation running near 6% through the first half of 2027, 
driven mainly by food prices after a weak monsoon and some spillover from higher input 
costs, before easing toward 4% by year-end, averaging around 5% for the year. Core edges 
up to over 4% but stays contained, although rising inflation expectations raise the risk of 
second-round effects. A return to normal harvests should bring headline inflation down in 
2028. In Brazil, El Niño-related food pressures, higher fuel prices, and rising labor costs 
from the move to a shorter working week will push inflation up, before those pressures 
fade. Mexico will make little headway, in our view, with persistent services inflation and 
the risk of peso weakness keeping inflation in the upper part of the tolerance range, rather 
than converging to target.

Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
25
Core vs Non-core Inflation Amidst Sluggish Supply Chains
Though the Middle East conflict remains unresolved, our commodity 
strategists and market pricing point toward de-escalation. Crude prices alone, 
however, give an incomplete picture. Refining spreads are historically wide, so the 
same price of crude can imply a higher cost of fuel than past relationships would 
suggest. Our commodity strategists expect diesel markets to remain unusually 
tight into mid-2027. Refinery constraints, delayed maintenance and higher shipping 
costs mean that better crude availability may not translate quickly into cheaper 
refined products. Even as crude prices fall, households and businesses may see less 
relief at the pump. Diesel matters well beyond fuel bills.

The divergence between oil prices and refined product prices is a key reason 
central banks are finding it hard to look through this oil price shock. The 
persistence of the shock is boosting producer prices in key segments: chemicals, 
rubber, plastics and transport, to name a few. Though consumer prices have 
responded less to date, goods inflation remains a core focus for inflation risks, 
especially given the surge in inflation caused by tariffs imposed by the Trump 
administration. The US has shown early signs of greater pass-through into core 
goods, but evidence of broad second-round effects remains limited. Falling energy 
prices should help curtail risks, but we would expect central banks to remain 
cautious until they have more proof.

Ultimately, demand will determine how far non-core inflation can push to core. 
Higher energy costs 
weaken real purchasing power even when firms cannot pass them on, and tighter 
monetary policy is now adding to the restraint. Consumption surprised to the 
upside in the first phase of the shock, but households with fewer savings and 
higher borrowing costs should find further price increases harder to absorb. More 
of the adjustment would then fall on business margins and volumes. Consequently, 
our baseline combines a hit to growth with a modest hiking cycle, followed by 
disinflation.
We see a clear risk that repeated supply shocks may change inflation 
expectations, psychology, and price formation. If households and companies 
begin to expect persistently higher inflation, wage and price setting could extend 
the shock well beyond energy. Central banks would then have to restrict aggregate 
demand even more to weaken growth and push inflation down. Our Rates and Oil 
downside scenario illustrates that more difficult sequence: inflation prompts 
further tightening before the full hit to demand becomes clear.  

M
Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
27
Monetary Policy 
Monetary policy in developed and emerging markets has differential constraints. In 
developed markets, persistent inflation keeps policy modestly restrictive, with our 
baseline being that the Fed, ECB and BoJ deliver a bit more tightening. Emerging markets 
are likely to be more split, with India tightening in response to persistent inflation 
pressures, Mexico gradually easing, and China keeping rates unchanged while relying on 
targeted support. Where central banks were hiking or on pause, we see a reconvergence 
towards an easing bias by 2028 as the differentiated effects of oil, diesel, and food finally 
pass through inflation.
In the US, resilient growth with persistent inflation keeps the Fed focused on preventing 
higher energy prices from generating second-round effects. We expect the Fed to raise the 
policy rate to 4.375%, a recalibration to tighter policy but not an aggressive hiking cycle. If 
inflation peaks as we expect in 1Q27, and disinflation eventually becomes well established, 
we think it will reverse some of that tightening by 2028, with the first cut in 2Q28 and 
measuredly taking rates to 3.625%.
For the ECB, elevated energy prices, resilient growth and broader pass-through into wages 
and services also prompt further tightening. We expect two 25bp hikes, in December 2026 
and March 2027, taking the policy rate to 3%; the ECB then pauses in restrictive territory 
as tighter financial conditions weigh on the outlook. Easing will begin in June 2028, we 
believe, once headline inflation is undershooting the 2% target and policymakers have 
greater conviction that core inflation is firmly declining, with quarterly cuts taking the 
policy rate to 2.25% by 4Q28. 
In Japan, we see the BoJ continuing its gradual normalization, judging that underlying 
inflation has reached its 2% target while financial conditions remain accommodative. We 
expect two 25bp hikes, in December 2026 and March 2027, taking the policy rate to 1.75%. 
Thereafter, we expect the BoJ to remain on hold as the pass-through from higher crude-oil 
prices fades, inflation settles below 2% from 2H27 and the lagged effects of previous 
tightening become more visible. With growth remaining around potential, we think the BoJ 
is likely to view the resulting policy stance as broadly neutral. 
The BoE remains on hold through 2027 after a February rate hike, with the energy shock 
keeping near-term inflation elevated even against a softer underlying growth backdrop. As 
those effects fade and both headline and core inflation move more convincingly toward 
target, we expect the BoE to begin cutting in 2028. The contrast with the Fed and ECB 
reflects a weaker underlying demand backdrop: absent the energy shock, softer growth 
and inflation would have argued for earlier easing.

China maintains a calibrated monetary policy stance. Exports provide enough of a cushion 
to aggregate growth that broad-based easing is not required, while elevated global rates 
further constrain the scope for policy-rate reductions. We expect the PBoC to keep rates 
broadly unchanged, relying instead on liquidity provision and targeted credit support. 
With fiscal policy also constrained by local-government debt pressures, policy support 
remains targeted rather than reflationary. 
Across emerging markets, we think policy paths will diverge as central banks balance 
domestic inflation pressures against softer growth and tighter global financial conditions. 
In India, broadening price pressures and concerns about second-round effects warrant 

M
Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
29
further tightening, and we expect the RBI to deliver three additional hikes, taking the 
policy rate to 6.25% by April 2027. 
In Brazil, the BCB will continue easing in 2028 after an extended hold for 2027, as de-
anchored inflation expectations and fiscal uncertainty constrain the pace of cuts. A 
credible multi-year fiscal consolidation plan could re-anchor expectations and create 
substantially more room for easing; absent such an adjustment, we expect rates to remain 
above neutral. 
Banxico also retains an easing bias. We expect further easing to take the policy rate to 
6.0% by March 2027, as domestic slack and low exchange-rate pass-through allow 
policymakers to ease independently of the Fed. Thereafter, persistent core services 
inflation and a gradual recovery in activity argue for an extended pause.

32
The Quest to Find Neutral
Resilient growth alongside higher interest rates has reopened the question of 
where the neutral policy rate lies. In theory, the real neutral policy rate, r*, 
neither stimulates nor restrains activity when output is at potential and inflation is 
at target. The trouble is, the neutral rate cannot be observed directly. Economists 
infer it from models and from the response of the economy to policy, making it a 
useful but uncertain benchmark for judging how restrictive interest rates are. 
Cross-currents from energy, consumer resilience and AI have made that judgement 
harder this year.
In the US, our work suggests that neutral has moved higher and that current 
policy is neutral to modestly restrictive. Our US economists approach that 
judgment in two ways. A New Keynesian framework uses output, unemployment, 
and inflation gaps to estimate the real short rate consistent with equilibrium. That 
model puts real neutral at about 1.5%, up from just below 1% in early 2025, 
equivalent to roughly 3.5% nominal with 2% inflation. That sits between the 
Holston-Laubach-Williams and Lubik-Matthes estimates, and a touch above the 
FOMC's 3.25% reference. A separate assessment of broader financial conditions 
finds restraint, but not an unusually large amount. Together, the evidence points to 
policy that is neutral to modestly restrictive.
Exhibit 49:
Our estimate of the real neutral rate of interest had risen by over 50bp to about 
150bp as of 2Q26. That said, confidence intervals are wide and misidentification of supply 
shocks (tariffs and oil) likely bias model estimates higher

Source: Morgan Stanley Research. Note: Two-sided current estimate is as of 2Q26. Expressed in terms of the real federal funds rate needed to keep the 
macro-economy in equilibrium.  
AI could change the medium-term destination. Our baseline builds in a modest 
productivity improvement (equivalent to about a quarter point of GDP growth 
per year); the productivity upside assumes a larger gain. Sustained increases in 
productivity would raise expected returns and investment demand, theoretically 
lifting neutral. Recent industry evidence is consistent with that possibility, but 
gains in highly exposed sectors do not yet establish an economy-wide TFP 
acceleration. Capital deepening and supply shocks complicate the inference, so the 
case for a higher neutral rate is stronger than our confidence in its precise level.
The debate extends beyond the US. The ECB's semi-structural, term-structure 
and DSGE (dynamic stochastic general equilibrium) models produce a nominal 
neutral range of 1.75-2.5%, with policy currently at the top end of the range. Our 

M
Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
33
estimate is modestly above 2%. The question is how much of the apparent increase 
will persist once the energy shock fades. The coming leadership transition could 
also change how the ECB communicates that uncertainty. In our baseline, the ECB 
raises rates to 3% before easing to 2.25% by end-2028 as inflation subsides.
Japan is approaching neutral from below. Three of the BoJ's six models cluster 
around a nominal rate of 1.5%, close to our own estimate. The economy is 
leaving behind decades of deflation, and the BoJ must judge whether positive 
nominal growth and underlying inflation are durable. Our Japan economists expect 
gradual hikes to 1.75% by March 2027, followed by a pause. The uncertainty 
concerns both the neutral rate and how quickly policy should reach it.

Policy is set to stay restrictive through 2027 as most central banks are expected 
to hold rates above neutral to contain second-round effects from the energy shock

Source: National central banks, Morgan Stanley Research. Note: ** Mid-point of the published range unless noted. Fed: median FOMC participant longer-run 
projection (range across participants). SARB: point estimate embedded in its Quarterly Projection Model; no range published. NBH: no official estimate; 
marker is the Morgan Stanley estimate (about 1% real plus the 3% target). Range of estimates computes using latest published estimates by central banks 
(for example for developed markets, see graph 1.4 here).
Away from the large economies, the central banks of Hungary and South Africa 
present interesting case studies; the former because of the goal of euro 
convergence and the latter from a change in central bank inflation target. In 
Hungary, slower potential growth has lowered returns on capital, while real 
exchange-rate appreciation associated with productivity convergence does some of 
the disinflation work. We estimate real neutral at about 1%. Real rates roughly 2pp 
above that level and a firm forint imply restrictive conditions. As EU funds and FDI 
lift potential growth, we expect neutral to rise toward 1.5% by 2030. For South 
Africa, the SARB's Quarterly Projection Model ties neutral to an equilibrium risk 
premium linked to government debt. Our MS SAMM framework gives more weight 
to potential growth and the external balance, producing an estimate below the 
SARB's nominal 5.5%. On that assessment, policy is tighter than the central bank 
assumes. 
For policymakers, a higher neutral rate shifts the destination more than the 
direction. Where the neutral rate has risen, terminal rates settle higher and easing 
cycles prove shallower. Where it is lower than assumed, policy is tighter than it 
looks. The common risk is tightening toward estimates inflated by supply shocks 
rather than by genuine demand.

M
Global Insight
34
Alternative Scenarios
Stronger growth can come from stronger demand or stronger supply, and the 
distinction matters for inflation and interest rates. Our two upside scenarios separate 
those forces. The downside scenarios examine a shortfall in AI investment and the 
stagflationary impulse from a more persistent oil shock that prompts further monetary 
tightening. Together, the four scenarios illustrate how the sources of growth, and the 
policy response to them, could change the outlook through 2028. 
In both upside cases, US growth moves toward 3%, compared with 2.5% in 2027 in our 
baseline. The key contrast in the upside scenarios is that stronger demand boosts inflation 
and prompts higher rates in the Aggregate Demand scenario, while faster productivity 
allows growth to strengthen without comparable price pressure in the AI Productivity 
scenario. 
In the other direction, weaker AI investment in one scenario lowers demand and inflation, 
giving central banks room to ease. In the other, higher oil prices create a harder choice: 
inflation rises first, and the combined weight of expensive energy and tighter policy 
subsequently pushes growth lower. We do not model a separate falling-oil, falling-rates 
scenario for two reasons: first, because our baseline follows the expectation of oil moving 
lower across the forecast horizon, in line with market pricing; and second, the outcomes 
would resemble a mix of the baseline and productivity upside scenarios, even if the supply 
/ demand drivers were through different mechanisms.

Stronger aggregate demand in the US helps support an already-strong AI-driven trade 
cycle and boosts growth globally, while AI adopters such as the UK, and AI-trade exposed countries like 
Korea and Taiwan benefit the most in a AI-productivity upside scenario. Higher energy prices and rates 
weigh on growth across most economies

Scenario 1: Aggregate Demand Upside
Aggregate demand outstrips expectations, driving inflation higher. AI investment and 
spending by high-income households in the US gathers steam, labor demand picks up, and 
income growth is broadly based. The labor market moves away from our "slow-hire/slow-
fire" equilibrium as unemployment falls to 3.5%, and the expansion begins to overheat.
The stronger spending builds price pressures. US GDP growth exceeds 3% in 2027 and 
2028, and although falling oil prices helps bring headline inflation lower, core inflation in 
the US stays just above 3% without making a clear turn lower over the forecast period. 
The Fed responds with further tightening, taking the federal funds rate to 5.625% at end-
2027 and 5.625% at end-2028, just above where it peaked in 2023/24. Put simply, growth 
is stronger, but so is the restraint needed from monetary policy.
Stronger US demand lifts exports and investment elsewhere, taking global growth to 3.9% 
in 2027 and 3.7% in 2028 (4Q/4Q). The euro area is a key beneficiary, with growth moving 
toward 2%, from around 1%, as exports help close the output gap faster. Inflation remains 
elevated through 2027 before moderating in 2028, and the ECB holds rates at 3.5% across 
the horizon. UK growth exceeds 2% by 2028, with inflation following a similar path to the 
euro area. The BoE raises rates to 5.5% in 2027 and holds them there in 2028.

M
Global Insight
36
Similarly, Japan benefits from strong US demand through exports, with growth reaching 
1.5% in 2028. Firmer wages and inflation expectations reinforce reflation, keeping inflation 
above target and taking BoJ rates to 2.5% by end-2028. In China, stronger exports and 
industrial activity help stabilize household demand after the extended deflationary cycle. 
Growth approaches 5% by 2028, although ample supply keeps inflation close to 1%, and 
the PBoC holds its policy rate at 1.25%. In India, stronger exports reinforce the domestic 
capex cycle, but higher inflation requires the RBI to raise rates toward 7%.
The stronger cycle helps Brazil navigate its near-term credit pressures, with growth 
recovering toward 2% by 2028. Inflation also reaccelerates, and the BCB has to keep real 
rates high, easing by only 100bp over 2027 and 2028.  Mexico benefits more directly from 
stronger US demand, taking growth above 2%. With inflation approaching 4%, Banxico 
ends its easing cycle and raises rates to 6.75% by end-2028.

Scenario 2: AI Productivity Upside
Faster productivity from AI adoption allows stronger growth with less inflation 
pressure. AI spending and high-income consumers continue to support demand, but AI 
delivers a larger productivity improvement – roughly half a percentage point annually. The 
labor market remains in its "slow-hire/slow-fire" equilibrium, with few signs of overheating 
despite resilient spending. Compared to our baseline, growth is faster but less inflationary. 
US growth moves above 3% by 2028, outperforming growth in the baseline, but the 
greater productive capacity allows inflation to return toward 2% without a demand 
slowdown. Despite less need for monetary restraint, the faster growth in productivity 
means that potential GDP growth is faster, and thus the equilibrium or neutral rate (r*) is 
also higher. The Fed recognizes the faster growth and is therefore reluctant to cut, but the 
moderating inflation prevents hikes, as well. The scenario table puts the federal funds rate 
at 4.375% at both end-2027 and end-2028, rather than falling in 2028 as it does in our 
baseline.
Europe benefits from stronger external demand and modest productivity gains. Euro area 
growth improves somewhat, while inflation falls below 2%, allowing the ECB to lower its 
policy rate toward 2% in 2028. The UK gets a larger productivity boost, supporting 
somewhat stronger growth than in the euro area. That also limits the extent of easing, 
with the Bank Rate at  4.25% by end-2028. In Japan, stronger exports lift near-term growth, 

M
Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
37
structural labor shortages support domestic investment, and productivity gains contain 
inflation. The scenario puts the BoJ policy rate at 2.0%.
In China, faster AI diffusion improves productivity, and in contrast to the developed world, 
the adoption disrupts the labor market and weakens employment. The softer path for 
labor income means weaker consumption growth, which partially offsets the external 
boost. Inflation remains below 1%, and policy stays highly accommodative. India gains 
more: growth improves by a similar amount to the demand upside, but without the same 
inflation pressure, allowing the RBI to cut rates.
Brazil and Mexico also reach growth outcomes broadly similar to the demand upside, 
with lower inflation and interest rates. In Brazil, stronger growth improves the fiscal 
backdrop, allowing the BCB to reduce nominal rates as inflation falls and to lower real 
rates as well. In Mexico, firmer activity similarly comes with less need for monetary 
restraint.

Scenario 3: AI Investment Downside
In stark contrast to our expectations, in this risk scenario AI investment falls outright 
in 2027 as power and construction bottlenecks delay the build-out. This scenario 
envisions a decline in spending, rather than simply slower growth from a high level – a key 
distinction relative to other scenarios where the level of spend remains elevated. Non-
residential business fixed investment contracts before recovering modestly in 2028. 
Weaker asset prices then weigh on confidence and consumption, extending the slowdown 
beyond the initial investment shock.
Household spending slows less sharply than investment, but takes longer to recover. As 
firms respond to weaker demand, the labor market softens and unemployment rises 
above 5%. US GDP growth slows to near 1%. Inflation falls below target by 2028, giving 
the Fed room to respond aggressively: the federal funds rate reaches 1.875% at end-2027 
and 1.375% at end-2028. We do not assume a return to the zero lower bound or renewed 
asset purchases.
The global spillovers are substantial, though less severe than in the Rates and Oil 
downside scenario. Global growth falls below 3% as weaker US demand reduces exports 
and AI-related investment elsewhere. Euro area growth slows toward 0.5%, inflation falls 
below target by 2028, and the ECB  cuts rates to 1.75% by 2027 and 0.75% by 2028.  UK 

M
Global Insight
38
growth is barely positive, with inflation also well below target and weaker growth 
requiring fiscal tightening; the BoE cuts the Bank Rate to 1.75%.
Japan’s structural labor shortages cushion the initial shock, but growth still falls toward 
zero. Inflation drops well below target, and the BoJ lowers rates below 1%. China uses 
domestic policy support to keep growth above 4%, although activity is weaker than in our 
baseline and inflation softer. India slows without a broader disruption to its domestic 
cycle.
The direct effects are smaller in Brazil and Mexico. Brazil's growth weakens only modestly 
because its exposure to AI investment is more limited, while softer inflation allows the 
BCB to cut rates. Mexico also sees a growth hit, given its tie to the US economy. The 
common mechanism is weaker demand and lower inflation, allowing monetary policy to 
cushion the slowdown.

Scenario 4: Rates and Oil Downside
In this scenario, hostilities in the Middle East resume, driving oil prices notably higher 
and rekindling shortages. Higher oil prices push inflation higher, weighing on economic 
growth and prompting tighter monetary policy; the larger hit to growth comes later. The 
oil price rises to $125-150/bbl, and the persistence of the shock allows higher energy costs 
to pass into core prices. Central banks respond to those second-round risks before the full 
effects on household spending and business investment become clear. The combined 
effects of the cost shock and the tight monetary policy create recessionary conditions.
In the US, inflation rises above its 2026 peaks during 2027. Consumers face higher energy 
bills and borrowing costs, while business spending slows. The scenario table takes the 
federal funds rate to 5.125% at end-2027. The accumulated pressure produces a mild 
recession late that year, leaving growth barely positive in 2028: GDP grows 1.6% in 2027 
and 0.3% in 2028.
Weak activity brings down inflation only over time, late in the forecast period. Given the 
renewed inflationary pressures, the Fed delays any policy easing to combat softer growth 
until after the disinflationary process has become clearly entrenched and the threats of 
second-round effects have ebbed. At the end of 2028, the policy rate has been reduced to 
3.125%. Persistent inflation limits how quickly policy can respond to the downturn. That 
delay makes this scenario more damaging to growth than a demand shock that gives 
central banks scope to cut immediately.

M
Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
39
Europe faces the same difficult sequence. Higher energy costs, weaker external demand, 
and tighter financial conditions produce recessionary outcomes in the euro area. Inflation 
is slow to respond to weaker activity, keeping ECB policy restrictive. The UK also falls into 
recession, while persistent inflation limits the BoE’s room to ease.
Japan faces weaker exports and consumption alongside higher energy costs and supply-
chain pressure. Growth falls to around zero in 2027 before stabilizing in 2028, while 
inflation constrains the BoJ’s response. China is relatively insulated from the direct oil 
shock, but weaker external demand and pressure on real incomes pull growth toward 4% 
in both years. CPI inflation falls after the initial oil-driven increase. In India, higher import 
costs weaken domestic demand and slow growth toward 6%.
Brazil’s combination of weaker external demand and tight domestic conditions leaves 
growth barely positive across the horizon. The BCB has to wait until later in the forecast 
period to cut rates as inflation recedes. Mexico feels the slowdown in US demand, taking 
growth below 1% in 2028. Across these economies, the difficulty is the timing: inflation 
requires restraint before growth weakens enough to warrant relief.

40
Fiscal Policy  
Deficits narrow only modestly in our baseline forecasts, even with help from higher 
nominal growth. While strong nominal growth has improved the near-term debt 
arithmetic, higher interest costs and little, if any, fiscal restraint limit that effect. In 
particular, elections, defense spending, and the AI build-out will shape individual budgets. 
For debt to stabilize, governments still need to bring deficits down. 
In the US, the deficit remains near 6% of GDP, but the modest boost to growth from fiscal 
policy fades: we see the fiscal impulse falling from roughly 40bp in 2026 to around 
neutral in 2027 as defense-led government spending offsets the drag from various benefit 
cuts for lower-income Americans in Medicaid and the Supplemental Nutrition Assistance 
Program (SNAP). We see some chance of the newly elected Democratic Congress delaying 
those cuts, which could result in a modest net boost to the fiscal impetus. For driving 
growth, it is the change in the deficit, not its high level, that matters.
AI investment is an important swing factor for US tax receipts. As OBBBA's front-loaded 
expensing deductions shrink in 2027, the effects of AI investment and associated wealth 
gains should become more visible in tax receipts. That, in turn, should keep the debt-to-
GDP ratio broadly stable over the next two years, but it will also leave the fiscal outlook 
exposed to an AI slowdown.

Euro area deficits widen in 2026 before stabilizing near 3.5% of GDP, on our forecasts, 
masking substantial differences across countries. We assume that Germany continues 
to expand spending, including on social, defense, and infrastructure programs; the level of 
spending stabilizes at a high level by 2028. France is the main pressure point: the deficit 
does not narrow, OAT spreads remain wide, and the ECB’s high bar for intervention leaves 
fiscal policy to do the adjustment. The presidential and possible parliamentary elections in 
2027 make consolidation difficult, although revenue measures could enter the 2027 or 
2028 budget. A growth slowdown would make the arithmetic harder by weakening 
receipts while borrowing costs remain high.

deferring some of the tightening. Gilt markets remain the constraint, but fiscal restraint 
itself weighs on growth. A return of inflation to target would offer the least costly way to 
reduce funding costs, in our view.

Across Asia, we think fiscal settings will change less, though the direction may differ 
by economy. We see Japan remaining moderately expansionary. China continues to 
withdraw support despite weak household demand and falling land revenues. The 
augmented deficit has already narrowed materially, and we assume that Beijing will favor 
targeted infrastructure bonds and quasi-fiscal tools over broad stimulus. Fiscal policy 
cushions activity without providing a broader reflation. India continues to consolidate in 
our baseline, reprioritizing spending to protect public capital expenditure and its deficit 
target despite higher subsidies. Korea's record 2027 budget extends its policy push, with 
semiconductor-related revenues limiting the fiscal cost.

Brazil is also a major focus for fiscal scrutiny in financial markets. Constraints from 
mandatory spending and indexation remain in effect, complicating the Presidential 
Congressional negotiation around fiscal resolutions. A modest credit downturn would add 
to the pressure, and elevated risk premia lead to some consolidation. Mexico has less 
remaining room to adjust after cuts to public investment have already done much of the 
work. We therefore expect its 2027 budget to set a flatter, more realistic consolidation 
path, supported by a broader corporate tax base.

Debt Sustainability: Holding its G≥Round, for Now 
Despite market focus on rising yields, DM debt sustainability has, on the whole, 
temporarily improved versus a year ago because of faster nominal growth. That 
conclusion comes from this year's DSA (Debt Sustainability Analysis) refresh (see: 
Sovereign Debt Holding its G≥Round). Effectively, the short-term outlook is better, 
while the medium-term outlook is worse because of upside surprises in nominal 
growth. Empirically, the benefit from strong nominal growth on debt metrics is 
immediate, whereas the back-up in yields only realizes as debt is refinanced, which 
takes more than five years in most economies. Technically, G≤R has become G≥R in 
the short run. DM debt-to-GDP in 2030 is now projected to be ~5pp lower than a 
year ago. Clients can stress-test debt trajectories for different economic 
outcomes with our new MS DSA Interactive Tool.
Exhibit 66:

Morgan Stanley Research
Despite stronger debt metrics, fiscal stability has not been attained. Overall,  
debt metrics remain quite poor versus historical standards. We began assessing 
debt metrics in 2024 with Flashing Yellow: DM Sovereign Debt & Fiscal 
Flashpoints, where we were cautious but not alarmed since fiscal positions were 
still consolidating. A year later, our work in DM Sovereign Debt, Losing G≤Rip 
argued that the risks were increasing because debt metrics were deteriorating. 
Nominal growth was softening faster than fiscal positions were consolidating, debt 
ratios were running several points above prior projections, and rising debt servicing 
costs were closing the R v G gap in several economies. Such a scenario is not far-
fetched, should nominal growth surprise to the downside instead of the upside. 
Fiscal consolidation will be required – it's not a question of if, but when. 
In the US, upside surprises in nominal growth make debt look more sustainable 
on a five-year horizon, but the long run has worsened. Debt holds near ~125% of 
GDP in 2026-27, even as our economists see the federal deficit widening above 6% 
of GDP. The strong nominal growth outcomes are also why we can see some room 
for budget negotiation in the upcoming Congressional session with a divided 
government. The size of the debt distracts from what matters more, namely the 
speed of accumulation and how it is financed. On that front, the Treasury's reliance 
on bills ahead of coupon increases next year shortens the maturity profile and 
quickens the pass-through of higher rates to fiscal deficits, posing risks to the 
medium-term outlook.

Within Europe, the fiscal tailwind from cheap debt and low defense spending 
has turned into a headwind, and the divergence is widening. Our Europe 
economists frame this as a shift from cheap debt to hard choices, as ageing, 
defense and interest costs compete for the same fiscal space. In that debate, 
growth is not one objective among many, but the entire problem. France is at the 
center of the focus here because its deficit is not consolidating despite market 
pressures. On current OAT yields, our economists see debt rising toward almost 
160% of GDP by 2040. The UK, by contrast, is undergoing one of the more 
serious consolidations in developed markets. The October Budget kept the 
planned move to a primary surplus intact, which puts debt on a declining path if 
delivered, and gilts have begun to trade more like a core market as fiscal risk 
premia compress.

Japan shows how much nominal growth can change the picture. Reflation has 
taken nominal growth from near zero to around 3%, keeping the effective interest 
cost well below growth. The debt ratio has declined for six consecutive years, the 
first such run in data back to 1997, and tax revenues continue to outpace higher 
interest costs. Our Japan economists therefore see fiscal concerns as overdone. 
The large debt stock still leaves Japan more sensitive to interest rates than other 
developed markets. The favorable gap between growth and interest costs is an 
opportunity to consolidate before refinancing erodes it, or weaker growth brings it 
to an end sooner.

Morgan Stanley Research
45
Trade  
AI investment will likely remain the main driver of global trade in 2027-28. Trade in 
semiconductors and compute hardware will continue to be massive. But spending on 
power infrastructure and other equipment means that trade should broaden even as 
growth (but not outright spending) in those original categories moderates. Given the rapid 
increase in chip pricing, the rapid increase in spending next year will create an even bigger 
wedge between real and nominal variables. Outside AI, we expect a resilient consumer 
backdrop to put a floor under goods trade, and defense and energy spending to support 
selected exporters. Industrial policy will also influence where production takes place. 
Overall, our Asia capex super-cycle story reflects the broadening out of global trade.

Importantly, trade 
and industrial 
policies have 
redirected trade 
flows. Tariff policies 
have created new 
incentives for goods 
to be redirected and 
re-sourced from 
different economies. 
These incentives are 
evident in trading 
patterns. As tariffs 
have stabilized in 
2026, we expect some trade patterns to revert. The USMCA agreement remains 
unresolved, and that will be a further catalyst for trade reorientation next year. One 
stated goal of the shift in US policy was reshoring, but to date, we have seen little 
evidence, when defined as a reduction in imports, on tariffed imports matched with an 
increase in domestic production. And even should reshoring gather pace, it could initially 
raise US imports. Building capacity requires equipment before it generates output. That is 
the reshoring J-curve: imports rise as the capital stock is built, with production following 
later. Manufacturing investment could therefore support global trade on multiple 
dimensions over the forecast horizon.
Asia's capex cycle extends beyond AI hardware. Investment in 
capacity, automation, power and supply-chain resilience continues 
despite expensive energy and trade uncertainty. Taiwan and Korea 
stand to benefit most directly from semiconductor demand, but 
regional investment will spread the gains. Japan supplies capital 
goods to Asian producers expanding capacity, so its exposure 
should extend beyond direct US demand. Southeast Asia benefits 
from assembly and supply-chain diversification.

China remains central to trade networks, even as final assembly moves elsewhere. 
Exports support growth amid weak domestic demand, with more Chinese value-added 
embedded in goods assembled across Asia. Our Asia team expects China’s share of global 
exports to rise from around 15% today to 16.5% by 2030. Manufacturing scale, supplier 
networks and engineering capacity support its move into higher-value industries.

Europe has an 
opportunity to 
participate more

broadens into power 
and other capital

Europe may capture less of the upside as its relative cost position weakens.
Industrial policy will continue to influence where that investment goes. Some tariff 
uncertainty may fade, but government involvement in technology, energy and strategic 
supply chains remains high. Semiconductor export controls, restrictions on critical 
minerals, tariffs, quotas and trade agreements all affect the location of capacity. The 
result is likely, we think, to be a more fragmented trade cycle, driven increasingly by 
investment.

Global Insight
48
The risks follow the same mechanisms as our alternative scenarios. Stronger demand 
could broaden the cycle faster. Renewed tariffs or strategic restrictions could delay 
investment and require further supply-chain adjustment. In the Rates and Oil downside 
scenario, weaker final demand slows conventional manufacturing capex and makes trade 
more dependent on AI and strategic spending. The AI Investment downside scenario 
strikes more directly at the main source of trade momentum and acts as a catalyst for the 
wider capex cycle. The question for the baseline is whether today’s concentrated 
investment boom will broaden enough to sustain trade as semiconductor growth 
normalizes.

Morgan Stanley Research
49
Global Trade Patterns Amidst AI, Tariffs, and Relocation
Trade flows have shifted much faster than productive capacity moved. Tariffs, 
trade agreements, and export controls reshaped US and global trade through 
2025-26. The question for 2027 is how much of that change turns into a sustained 
increase in US manufacturing capacity. Tariffs now appear legally durable, but with 
the effective rate well below the peak statutory rate in 2025. To date, the evidence 
of imports being replaced by domestic production remains limited. Our earlier 
assessment in Reshoring? Not Quite Yet still broadly holds.
Most of the improvement in US manufacturing remains concentrated in the AI 
build-out. AI-linked goods account for much of the pick-up in industrial production 
since 2025, alongside investment in data center structures and related grid and 
power transmission. Outside AI, activity is weaker, apart from an idiosyncratic 
aerospace recovery and a post-tariff recovery in autos. Even within AI, import 
penetration has risen because domestic capacity has not kept pace with demand.
Exhibit 80:
US manufacturing has risen considerably in nominal terms, but a large part of this 
is attributable to price effects. Industrial production has risen as well, but the pickup has largely 
been driven concentrated in the AI-build-out

Source: Fed, CENSUS, BLS, BEA, Morgan Stanley Research
Prices further complicate the picture. US output prices remain well above import 
prices, and nominal manufacturing shipments have risen much faster than real 
industrial production. Higher nominal sales therefore do not establish that 
domestic production has displaced imports. Across manufacturing as a whole, 
import penetration has increased.
Relocating complex supply chains takes time and will be expensive. We have 
argued that reshoring could initially raise capital-goods imports as firms rebuild 
the capital stock before new production comes online. That J-curve is not yet 
clearly visible. Capital-goods imports outside chips have barely recovered, core 
manufacturing construction remains subdued, and the CHIPS Act and IRA 
structures boom is only beginning to generate equipment investment, without a 
corresponding increase in production.
The immediate constraint is broader business investment. Tariff uncertainty has 
kept spending on hold through much of 2026. Higher rates, tighter financial 
conditions and a moderating consumer should continue to restrain non-AI capex 
early in 2027, and our US economists expect a more meaningful broadening only 
after key trade issues, such as USMCA, are resolved. Our baseline consequently 
assumes limited reshoring over the forecast horizon.

North American investment around trade reorientation depends on the USMCA 
review. Mexico and Canada await greater clarity on the rules governing the 
regional manufacturing base. Mexico has gained disproportionately from AI even as 
its non-AI exports have struggled. It now supplies roughly half of US server 
imports, supported by large chip imports from Taiwan. Those flows show how final 
assembly can move while production remains tied to Asian suppliers. Rules of 
origin, foreign investment in Mexico and the treatment of strategic supply chains 
will be central to the negotiations.
If investment broadens across North America, the initial benefit will extend beyond 
the region. Building capacity requires intermediate goods and equipment from 
China, Korea and Europe. More US reshoring could therefore support global trade 
in 2027-28 before reducing import dependence later. For now, that remains a 
source of potential upside rather than a broad shift already visible in the data.

Morgan Stanley Research
51
US Public Policy: What's Next?
Ariana Salvatore
• Where can Congress temper executive action? We have been of the view 
that policy uncertainty since January 2025 has been tied to executive, not 
legislative action. In our discussions with clients, we often get the sense 
that they expect key pillars of the Trump agenda – including tariffs and 
geopolitics, trade (particularly the USMCA review), and deregulation – to 
fade under divided government. However, we expect policy volatility to 
remain largely unchanged, as the president's authority here prevails 
(particularly on tariffs and de-regulation). On Iran, Congress can control 
defense funding, on de-regulation, its primary power is oversight 
(investigations, hearings, etc.), but we see little practical policy impact from 
a change in congressional power alone.
• We see scope for marginally higher tariffs from here. In January, we 
argued that a less aggressive tariff path would likely be pursued ahead of 
the mid-term elections, given the political focus on affordability. Post-
elections, we think certain tariff authorities could potentially be re-invoked: 
1) political concessions / foreign policy leverage, 2) deficit reduction, 3) 
nearshoring, and/or 4) trade deficit reduction. Key catalysts: the USMCA 
review for tariffs on Mexico/Canada, and Sec. 232 for sectors relevant to 
national security (industrial policy).
• US-China: our base case remains an extension of managed competition 
rather than a grand agreement. The Busan consensus has three expiring 
components in early January: the 178 Section 301 exclusions, the BIS 
Affiliates Rule suspension, and China's second-wave rare-earth controls. We 
expect remaining bilateral meetings this year to focus on this extension and 
potential rudimentary AI safety dialogue. China hawkishness is bipartisan, 
but we see mutual dependencies capping escalation. Watch the NDAA for 
the inclusion of export control legislation, including the MATCH Act.
• AI data center restrictions are a state-level story, not a national one. We 
do not expect a federal pause, given the national security framing and the 
possibility of a veto. Binding levers (interconnection, rate design, siting, 
permitting) sit with state commissions, legislatures and county boards. Risk 
is concentrated in Texas and Ohio, but governors are unlikely to disrupt 
projects under construction, so we maintain our 2027 capex forecasts. We 
shift 5GW of US projects from 2028 to 2029, leaving US data center capex 
flat from 2027 to 2028, then up ~40% in 2029. We see a delay, not a 
pause, given that 1) political limitations temper campaign ambitions; 2) 
projects under construction or with permits approved permits are likely to 
be insulated; and 3) there are various mitigation efforts under way by the 
hyperscalers.

M
