Country Snapshots
US: Resilient Growth, Reluctant Disinflation – We expect the US expansion to stay 
resilient through 2027, with structural support from AI-related capex and wealth effects 
that underpin upper-income consumption. But cyclical factors are less supportive: fiscal 
stimulus fades and tighter policy weighs on rate-sensitive activity. Inflation moderates 
only reluctantly, keeping the Fed focused on measured recalibration. We expect two more 
25bp hikes, taking the target range to 4.25-4.50% by March 2027, then a hold through 
year-end.
Euro Area: Steady Growth, High Stakes – Despite headwinds from energy prices and 
higher yields, we see a steady pace ahead for euro area growth, close to potential. The 
economy benefits from supportive public demand, on the back of the German fiscal 
package, EU funds and defense spending. AI is also positively impacting EA trade, 
investment and productivity. Headline inflation will move further up and the diffusion to 
food and core inflation will strengthen. In that context, we foresee two additional ECB 
hikes in December and March. The ECB could start easing policy in June 2028 when the 
adverse inflation shocks fade. Elections in Spain, France and Italy have the potential to re-
shape the political landscape of the region.
Japan: Stronger Foundations, Near-Term Headwinds – Structural tailwinds remain, 
supported by a stronger nominal GDP growth trend, although private consumption 
continues to face near-term headwinds. We expect the BoJ to raise the policy rate to 1.75%, 
while significant uncertainty around the global economy creates two-sided risks to 
growth, inflation, and the policy rate path.
UK: Tipping Point(s) – Fiscal consolidation, capex growth, private-sector balance sheet 
clean-up and labor market rebalancing leave the UK in a robust position relative to its DM 
peers. A dissipating energy shock eventually brings in mildly less restrictive policy 
settings. Paired with the positive effects of AI adoption, this leads to a robust growth 
momentum build-up – albeit after a likely soft patch at the turn of the year. We see the 
BoE hiking twice – in December and February – and then remaining on hold until cuts in 
2028.
China: Lowflation Lingers, New Hope Takes Shape – China remains in a prolonged 
economic transition. We expect real GDP growth to moderate in 2027. Robust exports 
leave room for continued fiscal consolidation, while household deleveraging caps 
consumption, pushing nominal GDP growth below 5%Y. From 2028, easing drags from 
balance sheet stress and broader tech diffusion should support macro stabilization.
India: Growth Near Trend; External Risks in Focus  – We expect India's momentum to 
remain resilient as growth normalizes towards trend, supported by cyclical pick-up in 
domestic and external demand. Macro stability risks remain in check, supported by 
prudent and calibrated fiscal and monetary policy. Risks to outlook are balanced.
Brazil: Growth Weakens as Inflation Keeps Rate Relief on Hold – We expect a deeper 
growth slowdown in 2027, as stretched household balance sheets, fading labor market 
resilience and a smaller fiscal impulse weigh on domestic demand. Meanwhile, food, fuel 
and policy-related cost pressures should lift inflation, keeping it above target despite 
weaker activity. Heightened fiscal uncertainty and de-anchored inflation expectations

should keep the BCB on hold through end-2027, before easing begins in 2028. Lower rates 
should support a mild recovery in consumption and investment, but monetary policy 
should remain restrictive. Delivering a sustained fiscal adjustment remains key to 
containing risks as public debt continues to rise.
Mexico: Cyclical Recovery on a Fiscal Tightrope – Easing trade uncertainty should 
support a recovery in investment and growth through 2027-28. Resilient US demand – 
including for IT and AI-related exports – helps, but spillovers remain limited. Keeping 
investment grade requires fiscal discipline, increasing the importance of PPPs for 
infrastructure. Inflation stays above target as services pressures persist and currency and 
weather risks complicate disinflation. We expect a final Banxico cut in March 2027.

US: Resilient Growth, Reluctant Disinflation
Michael Gapen
(1 212) 761 0571 
US Economics Team
We expect the US expansion to stay resilient through 2027, with structural 
support from AI-related capex and wealth effects that underpin upper-income 
consumption. But cyclical factors are less supportive: fiscal stimulus fades and 
tighter policy weighs on rate-sensitive activity. Inflation moderates only 
reluctantly, keeping the Fed focused on measured recalibration. We expect two 
more 25bp hikes, taking the target range to 4.25-4.50% by March 2027, then a 
hold through year-end. 
The US enters 2027 with structural support and cyclical headwinds. AI investment, its 
wealth spillovers, and improving productivity offset elevated energy prices, tighter policy, 
fading fiscal stimulus, and restrictive immigration. We expect real GDP growth of 2.5% 
4Q/4Q in 2027, little changed from 2.6% in 2026, before a modest pick-up to 2.7% in 
2028, with an investment-led expansion and resilient but less buoyant consumption. 
AI remains central. We expect non-residential fixed investment to rise 6.9% in 2027, after 
9.3% in 2026. Hyperscaler capex continues to rise rapidly, with some moderation in 
growth; but the domestic GDP impulse is smaller than gross spending suggests, as much 
AI equipment is imported. AI's contribution to growth should peak around 2027, before 
the cycle rotates from build-out towards broader adoption from 2028. 
Outside AI, investment is weaker. We expect that higher rates and energy costs interrupt 
the recovery in non-AI business spending, while residential investment stays flat with 
mortgage rates near 7% and the lock-in effect. Trade subtracts about 0.6pp from GDP 
growth in 2027 and 2028 as AI capex sustains capital-goods imports, and evidence of 
genuine reshoring remains limited.  
Consumer spending has been resilient despite weak real income growth, supported by 
wealth. Household net worth reached roughly $195tn in 2Q26, about $55tn above five 
years ago. We estimate wealth effects added around 0.6pp to real consumption growth 
over the four quarters through 3Q26. Our estimate of slowing real consumption growth 
(from 2.4% in 2026 to 2.0% in 2027) is largely due to soft real income growth and fading 
fiscal support. The saving rate is already trending lower, real income growth is forecast to 
recover only gradually, and fiscal policy is expected to turn into a drag as OBBBA support 
fades and SNAP/Medicaid cuts begin. Upper-income households stay supported by wealth, 
while low- and middle-income households drive the slowdown. Stronger real wages allow 
consumption to broaden again by 2028. 
The labor market stays "slow-hire, slow-fire". Restrictive immigration and demographics 
have cut the payroll breakeven to ~50k per month, while tighter policy, uncertainty, and 
AI substitution keep hiring muted. We expect payrolls to average about 35k per month in 
2027 and 2028, with unemployment rising from 4.2% at end-2026 to 4.3% in 2027 and 
4.4% in 2028. Decelerating wage growth indicates little sign of labor market overheating.

Global Insight
Morgan Stanley Research
73
AI displacement is more visible, but has been  a micro rather than macro story. 
Employment is weaker in highly exposed industries and among younger workers, but the 
broader market has absorbed the losses. AI is lifting productivity mainly through capital 
deepening rather than broad labor substitution, with trend productivity growth at about 
2.1% and rising, supporting solid growth without labor-market overheating. 
Inflation moves lower in 2027, but slowly. We expect core PCE to decline from 3.2% at 
end-2026 to 2.6% at end-2027, with headline at 2.3%. Tariff payback, softer airfares, and 
easing shelter support disinflation, but energy and transportation costs, sticky healthcare 
inflation, and AI-related demand for goods, power, and commodities limit progress. The 
result is disinflation, but a reluctant one.
We expect the Fed to pursue a measured recalibration rather than an aggressive hiking 
cycle: 25bp hikes in December and March, taking the range to 4.25-4.50%, then a hold 
through 2027. With our real neutral rate estimate up to around 1.5%, there is less room 
for eventual easing than before the pandemic; we expect 75bp of cuts in 2028, to 3.50-
3.75%. Balance-sheet reform may become more prominent, with a smaller Fed footprint 
under an ample-reserves framework. 
Fiscal policy stays supportive in level terms but adds little impetus in 2027. We expect 
the federal deficit to narrow only modestly, from 6.2% of GDP in 2026 to 5.9% in 2027, as 
higher defense and government spending partly offset the fading OBBBA impulse. Fiscal 
policy should be roughly neutral for growth after adding around 40bp in 2026. 
Risks are two-sided, and the source of any surprise matters for inflation and the Fed. 
Stronger demand could lift growth above 3% and keep inflation near 3%, requiring 
substantially more tightening, while a faster AI-productivity dividend would allow stronger 
growth with faster disinflation. On the downside, renewed Middle East escalation with oil 
at $125-150/bbl could bring stagflation and a mild recession, and an abrupt AI investment 
slowdown would hit capex, asset prices, and consumption together, prompting aggressive 
Fed easing.

Euro Area: Steady Growth, High Stakes
Jens Eisenschmidt
(49 69) 2166 2817
European Economics Team  
Despite headwinds from  energy prices and higher yields, we see a steady pace 
ahead for euro area growth, close to potential. The economy benefits from 
supportive public demand, on the back of the German fiscal package, EU funds 
and defense spending. AI is also positively impacting EA trade, investment and 
productivity. Headline  inflation will move further up  and the diffusion to food 
and core inflation will strengthen. In that context, we foresee two additional 
ECB hikes in December and March. The ECB could start easing policy in June 
2028 when the adverse inflation shocks fade. Elections in Spain, France and 
Italy have the potential to re-shape the political landscape of the region.  
Growth |  Headwinds and tailwinds.  Elevated energy prices and higher yields are 
significant headwinds, which will slow consumption and investment. And Europe 
continues to suffer considerable losses in export  market shares vis-à-vis China. However, 
the economy was also more resilient than we thought in the first half of 2026, and it will 
remain supported by structural tailwinds. First, fiscal policies are  supportive in 2026-27 as 
the German fiscal package is deployed, defense spending increases and countries such as 
Italy, Spain and Greece  still benefit from EU funds.  Second, AI diffusion is  having a positive 
impact on euro area trade, investment and productivity. In 2028, as adverse shocks are 
likely to  fade, we see private demand gaining momentum, which will be needed to offset a 
likely lesser impulse on growth from public demand. 
Steady, after all. With that, we see euro area GDP growth continuing at a broadly 
unchanged pace throughout our forecast horizon, and close to its potential. Quarterly 
growth could be a touch slower in the next quarters because of the adverse shocks from 
energy and yields, but gain slight momentum around mid-2027 when the shocks fade. It 
could then slow  again a tad in 2028 when fiscal policies become less supportive.
Inflation | Still climbing. The  elevated level of oil (diesel) and natural gas prices means 
headline inflation will rise further. It stood at 4.0%Y in October and will likely move close 
to 4.5% at the start of next year, we believe. While signs of indirect and second-round 
effects have been scarce so far, we foresee  some increase in core and food inflation in the 
coming months, as past episodes show evidence of lagged transmission of energy costs 
along production  chains. Core inflationary pressures are likely to remain  well below 2022-
23 levels (core peaked at 5.7%Y)  in a context where some  wage disinflation is still 
ongoing, demand is softer than in the post-Covid period, and supply tensions (to date) 
remain contained.  Core inflation could  be on a descending path as early as the second half 
of 2027, and more firmly so in 2028. At that horizon,  base effects from energy would also 
push headline HICP below 2%. 
Fiscal policy | Supportive national and EU fiscal policies. We project a significant 
increase in the euro area public deficit  in 2026-27, and fiscal policies will be supportive of 
growth.  This mostly reflects two factors. First, the German fiscal package is well under

75
way, with a deficit that  will increase  by 100bp in 2026 and 50bp again in 2027 in that 
country. Second, EU funds from NGEU-RFF continue to arrive in countries such as Italy, 
Spain and Greece. EU payments will end in December 2026, but significant lags in 
implementation mean the support to activity will likely be sustained next year.  In 2028, 
the level of fiscal support across the region will remain meaningful, but it will not increase 
further, in our view: its impact on growth is thus likely to be less. 
ECB | Rate hikes to 3.0%. We expect the ECB to deliver two more rate hikes  up to  3.0%, 
as energy prices remain elevated and growth resilient. With policy rates in restrictive 
territory, the central bank may then pause as tightening financial conditions weigh on 
economic activity. The inflationary impacts from energy prices should start to fade in 
2H27, and headline inflation fall below target in 1Q28. As  core inflation continues on a firm 
deceleration, we see the  ECB embarking on a cutting cycle. We expect three rate cuts, 
starting in June 2028 on a quarterly basis, down to 2.25%. 
Balance sheet.  We expect passive QT of APP and PEPP portfolios to continue and lead to 
a gradual decline in the ECB's balance sheet. The take-up for regular refinancing 
operations should start picking up more throughout 2027 as excess liquidity (excluding 
refinancing operations)  declines below €2.0tn from 4Q26 onwards. The Operational 
Framework review is expected to take place in 2027, and pave the way for structural 
refinancing operations, which could be introduced from 1Q28. 
Risks | Elections in Europe, and global factors. 50% of the euro area population will vote 
in the next months, with  elections in Spain, France and Italy. All eyes will be on France, 
where we expect the economy to lag the rest of the bloc. Our four global risk scenarios 
have important implications for the euro area. Stronger demand could push the ECB 
higher, for longer. Conversely, AI investment falling short of expectations would mean 
lower growth and the ECB normalizing policy more rapidly. On the supply side, a more 
pronounced AI productivity boost could deliver more growth with less inflation. Lastly, 
the more difficult shock to navigate for the ECB would come with higher energy prices 
and higher yields: inflation would increase more but the economy would stagnate.

Japan: Stronger Foundations, Near-Term Headwinds
Takeshi Yamaguchi
(81 3) 6836 5404 
Masayuki Inui
(81 3) 6836 5407
Structural tailwinds remain, supported by a stronger nominal GDP growth 
trend, although private consumption continues to face near-term headwinds. 
We expect the BoJ to raise the policy rate to 1.75%, while significant uncertainty 
around the global economy creates two-sided risks to growth, inflation, and 
the policy rate path. 
Stronger structural foundations. Japan's nominal GDP has returned to a sustained 
growth path after decades of stagnation under deflation. Rising prices and wages are 
supporting corporate profits, employee income, and tax revenues, while structural labor 
shortages, AI-driven innovation, and still-low real interest rates are encouraging labor-
saving investment. Despite constraints from construction costs and labor shortages, 
underlying investment demand remains strong in AI and data centers, semiconductors, 
power infrastructure, and supply-chain resilience. As this demand materializes, capex 
growth should gradually lift productivity and potential growth. Barring a dissolution of 
the House of Representatives and a snap election, no national elections are scheduled for 
2027, supporting policy continuity and investment.
Nominal growth supports fiscal improvement. Stronger nominal income and corporate 
profits have driven a substantial increase in tax revenues. Meanwhile, because the 
effective average interest rate on government debt rises with a lag to market rates, 
nominal growth should continue to exceed the government's effective funding cost for the 
time being. Non-tax revenues, including transfers from the Foreign Exchange Fund Special 
Account and the BoJ's transfers to the national treasury, should also improve the fiscal 
balance, although their size and sustainability warrant attention. Growing demand for 
retail JGBs should facilitate stable debt absorption, while the government has become 
increasingly attentive to JGB market conditions.
Slower real wage growth weighs on near-term consumption. Despite these positive 
shifts in the underlying foundations of the economy, in the near term, we expect 
household spending to remain under pressure through 1H27, as cost-push inflation in 
energy, food, and durable goods slows real wage growth. Cautious spending behavior is 
likely to persist, particularly among middle-aged workers with relatively modest wage 
gains and pensioner households. Reduced fuel subsidies and payback from front-loaded 
durable goods demand will add to the pressure. The consumption tax cut on food in April 
2027 should provide some relief, but we do not expect a full-fledged recovery in private 
consumption until 2H27.
Strategic and security-related investment to expand. Multi-year public and private 
investment in the government's 17 strategic sectors is set to ramp up from FY27, 
catalyzing private investment and supporting potential growth, economic security, and 
defense capabilities. Many of these sectors overlap with security-related areas, including 
AI and semiconductors, cybersecurity, quantum technology, aviation and space, 
shipbuilding, critical minerals, and energy security. With a new defense spending target

77
due by year-end, the focus will be both on the size of the increase and the scope of 
expenditures classified as security-related. Despite higher spending, we expect the debt-
to-GDP ratio to remain on a downward trend, supported by higher tax revenues from 
nominal growth and non-tax revenues.
Japan-style core inflation to temporarily exceed 3% YoY. We expect Japan-style core CPI 
inflation to temporarily exceed 3% YoY, due mainly to higher electricity charges and 
reduced fuel subsidies. However, falling rice prices and weak consumption should limit 
price pass-through, making a sustained rise above 3% unlikely. Higher gasoline prices could 
nevertheless raise inflation expectations and transportation costs, prolonging cost-push 
inflation. The impact of the consumption tax cut on food on household inflation 
expectations remains highly uncertain.
The end of the BoJ hiking cycle is approaching. We expect 25bp hikes in December 2026 
and March 2027, taking the BoJ's policy rate to 1.75%. With consumption weak, import-
driven price pass-through fading, and the weighted-average real interest rate turning 
positive, we expect the BoJ to remain on hold thereafter. Risks remain two-sided. Stronger 
wages, services inflation, inflation expectations, or renewed yen depreciation could push 
the BoJ's policy rate above 2%. Conversely, an AI investment correction that triggers a 
global slowdown and weighs on profits, capex, and wages could end the BoJ's 
normalization cycle earlier and bring rate cuts into play.
A wider range of risks. Risks around the growth outlook remain wide. Easing Middle East 
tensions and lower oil prices could improve Japan's terms of trade and household real 
income, reinforcing the virtuous cycle between wages and consumption. On the downside, 
with non-AI exports remaining sluggish, Japan's export growth is becoming increasingly 
dependent on AI-related demand. A sharper-than-expected slowdown in global AI capex 
could therefore weigh significantly on exports, production, capex, and corporate profits.

UK: Tipping Point(s)
Bruna Skarica  
(44 20) 7425 9110 
Fiscal consolidation, capex growth, private-sector balance sheet clean-up and  
labor market rebalancing  leave the UK in a robust position relative to its DM 
peers. A dissipating energy shock eventually brings in mildly less restrictive 
policy settings. Paired with the positive effects of AI adoption, this leads to a 
robust growth momentum build-up – albeit  after a likely soft patch at the turn 
of the year. We see the BoE hiking twice – in December and February – and 
then remaining on hold until cuts in 2028. 
Growth | An economy remade? GDP growth slows at the turn of the year, as household 
savings rate ticks up amid persistently high inflation and tighter financial conditions. AI 
and tech capex have, we estimate, driven up to one-third of UK growth since 2024, with 
the impact particularly pronounced in 2026. The UK looks to be the global AI adoption 
champion. We think the robust high-value added services growth can persist, with 
business capex boosting growth by an average 40bp over our forecast horizon. The 
cyclical productivity growth uptick over 2025 and 2026 likely reflects some structural 
strengthening, so we peg potential GDP growth at ~1.75% over 2027 and 2028, above the 
OBR's and BoE's estimates (~1.5%Y). As the energy shock dissipates and financial 
conditions normalize a touch, we expect private consumption growth to firm, boosted in 
2028 by both a lower savings rate and higher real disposable income growth. The 
contribution of the state to growth peaked in 2025, and is likely to slow further from 
here, we believe. We see risks to our growth forecasts as balanced.
Labor market | Watch this space. The build-up of slack has slowed, amid softer labor 
supply growth and a stabilization in the pace of headcount reduction. The labor market is 
still loose, and the improvement in hiring intentions relatively modest. While the data are 
mixed and warrant careful monitoring, it appears AI adoption has adversely  impacted 
graduate hiring, but with tentative evidence of improving payrolls in high AI-exposed 
sectors (ex-tech) and rising demand for AI skills. Survey data suggest pay growth at ~3.5% 
next year, which appears target-consistent amid recent productivity gains. As labor 
demand improves through 2H27 and 2028, we are sceptical of fast-paced labor market 
tightening, given a large reserve of UK-born workers in inactivity. Downside risk to our 
view is  a faster-than-expected drop in immigration flows.
Inflation | Once more unto the breach. The scale of the energy shock implies a year 
above the inflation target, although we see risks as firmly skewed to the downside over 
2H27, given ample scope for oil and gas prices to normalize. Inflation will peak at ~4.25%Y 
in 1Q27, we think. We see minimal second-round effects, given no signs of a material 
inflection higher in the year-ahead pay settlement surveys, and considering limited 
corporate pricing power. Indirect effects, however, could be more material than we are 
forecasting; we see upside risks to our travel-related and core goods price inflation 
forecasts. We see food inflation at peaking just above 4%Y next year, with very modest

risks around that forecast, given wholesale costs over 2H26. 
BoE | Neutral on neutral. We think the BoE is likely to deliver two more hikes, in 
December and February. Should it be able to forecast lower near-term inflation forecasts 
at its February meeting, we would expect the hike to come paired with messaging around 
a  prolonged period with rates in moderately restrictive territory. As inflationary dynamics 
normalize into 2028, we see the Bank removing restrictiveness in a gradual fashion, with 
the end-2028 Bank Rate at 3.75% in our baseline scenario. Across the scenarios outlined 
by our global team, and applying our subjective probabilities, our mean Bank Rate path is a 
bit higher than our modal forecast. Our thinking on neutral has shifted upwards, largely as 
our US research team upped its assessment of neutral, and as we have grown a bit more 
confident about the sustainability of the recent uptick in productivity growth. At the start 
of the easing cycle in 2024, we saw the neutral rate at ~3%, whereas we now peg it at 
~3.50%/3.75%. 
Fiscal policy | How do you like me now? The UK's consolidation continues. We see the 
primary deficit in FY26/27 at 0.9% of GDP (OBR forecast = 1.0% of GDP) and at 0.2% in 
FY27/28. Our forecasts assume a touch more robust tax receipts than the OBR's, given the 
delta in our labor market forecasts. Despite solid progress in reducing the primary deficit, 
the headline deficit remains elevated due to high debt servicing costs. Based on our rate 
strategists' gilt yield forecasts, we pencil in ~£10bn of a drop in interest payments in 
FY28/29, which helps push the headline deficit to 3% of GDP, despite a likely top-up in 
departmental spending budgets in next year's Spending Review. While the macro 
environment is uncertain, debt servicing costs are high, and a primary surplus has to be 
attained to arrest the rise in debt-to-GDP ratio, the UK has made good progress in 
consolidating already. The longer the delivery of balanced primary budgets persists, the 
more likely it is that the market sentiment around gilt yields will turn more notably.

Germany: Growth Peak in Sight
Claire Thürwächter
(49 69) 2166 2842
Public spending, robust private consumption and the global capex cycle boost 
growth in 2027. As the  fiscal impact fades, we expect a slowdown  in 2028.
Growth gathers pace. GDP grew at an average quarterly annualized rate of 1.3% through 
3Q26, exceeding expectations. We forecast full-year growth of 1.1%, following 0.3% in 
2025, and a further acceleration to 1.3% in 2027. Growth should ease towards potential in 
late 2028, but positive carry-over keeps the annual rate at 1.1% ( Exhibit 120 ).
Strong public consumption and exports. The strength is mostly attributable to two 
factors. First, government consumption, particularly social security spending, has 
expanded. Higher defense spending is also lifting public investment, while stronger civil 
engineering activity suggests the infrastructure fund is starting to feed through. Across all 
government entities, we estimate the fiscal expansion could add 50bp to growth in 2026 
and 30bp in 2027. This support should fade in 2028 as central government borrowing 
remains broadly unchanged ( Exhibit 121 ). The second growth driver is exports, which have 
benefited from rerouted demand (especially chemicals) and the global capex cycle. The 
latter should continue to support growth in 2027, potentially decelerating in 2028. 
Private investments a weak spot. Despite the tailwinds, Germany's  structural growth 
weakness persists. In particular, the loss of global market share limits the export upside, 
and gains  in some sectors  (such as defense and AI capex) are partly offset by weakness in 
other sectors (for example, autos). This manifests  in subdued private investment, which 
lags other countries and runs more than 10% below the 2019 level.
Deficit widening slows.  The fiscal expansion is clearly visible in a sizable deficit widening. 
We forecast the general government deficit (federal + regional + municipal + social 
security) to rise to 4.0% in 2026 from 3.0% of GDP in 2025 and then 4.5% in 2027. We 
forecast the widening to slow to 10bp in 2028. Defense-related borrowing should still 
rise, but infrastructure fund borrowing is likely to reach its annual target, while other 
headroom could tighten ( Exhibit 122 ). Central government borrowing therefore remains 
broadly unchanged in 2028, with some deficit widening potentially from social security or 
regional governments.
Reforms (still) in the making. The government's reform agenda has taken further shape. 
Some measures were legislated, proposals were made, and commissions are working on 
further areas. The shift towards capital-backed pension savings has attracted particular 
market attention: once fully phased in, we estimate that it could generate €90bn in 
additional annual inflows. Draft laws for the pension reform are being prepared and 
legislation could start in 2027, which should leave sufficient time for a 2028 phase-in, in 
line with initial plans. We continue to monitor  ongoing  efforts and view  the broader policy 
agenda as key to  whether the fiscally-induced rebound can be sustained by lifting 
potential growth.

France: A Decisive Year
Jean-François Ouvrard
(49 69) 2166 2813
The French economy enters election year with low momentum. We expect 
inflation to rebound towards 3% and growth to stay  below potential. Fiscal 
consolidation  is absent so far, and all eyes are on the April 2027 presidential 
election (and likely subsequent parliamentary elections in June 2027).
All eyes on the 2027 elections. The presidential election will be held on 18 April and 2 
May, and parliamentary elections could perhaps follow in June. If so, only next summer 
will we know whether the next President has won a majority, needs to build a coalition or  
faces a hung parliament. We recall that, in previous elections, polls have moved 
meaningfully in the final weeks before the vote (here). From today's perspective, the 
outcome of the election is still very uncertain. 
No progress on the deficit in 2027… Public finances will  be a key topic of the election as 
France's fiscal sustainability  is challenged: on our estimates, with current yields, it would 
require  a deficit reduction of 5pp of GDP or €150bn to get to the point where the debt 
would stabilize. We do not see that point being approached within our forecast horizon. 
For 2027, we expect the budget process to be resolved around mid-December either 
through Article 49.3 or ordinances. However, we expect the measures implemented to 
only stabilize the deficit at 5.4% of GDP. 
…and limited room for manoeuvre in 2028. For 2028, the fiscal trajectory will be a 
function of the election outcome. However, our sense is that the actual outcome may not 
differ that much, depending on the outcome of the election. Assuming the next 
government will want to show some willingness to reduce the deficit, and in a context 
where spending cuts are difficult to implement, the fastest path to a (slightly) lower 
deficit in 2028 will likely be through higher taxes. This is our baseline, under many 
scenarios. 
Low growth today… As of today, it also seems unlikely that growth will help much on the 
fiscal front. We expect French GDP growth of just 0.3%Y in 2026, well below the euro-
area average. We flag two key factors behind this subdued performance. First, the  labor 
market is deteriorating, with the unemployment rate up to 8.3% in 2Q26,  from a low of  
7.2% in early  2023, which weighs on consumption.  Second,  investment is declining; we 
suspect the uncertainty around policies, and tax measures  in particular, is weighing on 
household and business decisions. 
…and below potential until 2028. From here, macro headwinds will likely not abate 
much, in our view. We see inflation at a peak of 3.7% early next year, while higher yields 
are likely to be a drag on investment, particularly in construction. Political uncertainty will, 
at best, diminish only after any parliamentary  elections in June. With that, we see growth 
continuing well below potential (which we assume at 1.1%Y for France) in 2027-28.

Italy: More Headwinds than Tailwinds
Chiara Zangarelli
(44 20) 7677 3644
Growth is set to halve as consumption and RRF-driven investment slow. The 
labor market should cool while the deficit widens. Polling indicates continued 
support for Meloni's coalition.
Domestic demand | Slowdown ahead. We expect Italy's GDP growth to reach 0.8%Y in 
2026, before falling to 0.5%Y in 2027 and 0.4%Y in 2028. The main adjustment comes 
from domestic demand, led by a slowdown in private consumption. Italian households 
have not yet been fully compensated for the real-income shock and have so far supported 
consumption by reducing their saving rate to below pre-pandemic levels, making Italy the 
only one among the euro area's big four economies to do so. We expect the saving rate to 
fall further, partly cushioning the hit from higher oil and gas prices this winter. However, if 
households were more conservative and kept their saving rate at pre-pandemic levels, 
growth would be materially weaker than in our baseline, all else equal. 
Investment | RRF ending. The second leg of the domestic-demand correction comes from 
the end of the Recovery Fund programme in 2026. The programme has pushed non-
residential construction up by more than 100% since end-2019.  With the programme 
ending this year, much of the impulse is behind us. However, substantial funding remains 
to be spent, including the final tranche due by end-2026, so we expect the bulk of the 
correction only in 2027, implying a 50bp drag on GDP. 
Labor market | Starting to slow. We flagged in our mid-year outlook that Italy's labor 
market was not as strong as it appeared. Weak labor-force growth had pushed 
unemployment to unusually low levels, below the euro area. Now that labor-force growth 
is recovering, the unemployment rate has started to rise, increasing from a low of 5.5% at 
the start of 2026 to 6.2% in September. We expect slower employment growth, alongside 
a recovery in labor supply, to keep the unemployment rate above 6% all the way to 2028. 
Fiscal | Wider deficit next year. Based on the government's projections and our 
conversations in Rome (see here), Italy is expected to record a deficit of 2.9% of GDP in 
2026, paving the way for an exit from the EDP in 2027 and the activation of the National 
Escape Clause (NEC). The government has now revised up its deficit projections to 3.4% of 
GDP in 2027, from 2.8% in April, and to 3.2% in 2028, from 2.5% previously. The widening 
is largely driven by the use of the NEC for both defense and energy, adding 30bp of GDP 
to spending in each area per year. While we see risks of a wider deficit, we forecast a 
deficit of 3.4% of GDP next year, 50bp wider than the 2.9% expected in 2026. 
Politics | The 2027 elections. Election timing remains uncertain; we see either an April-
June window or a September election. At the time of writing, polling suggests that the 
incumbent coalition would be well positioned to secure a second term (see here), 
particularly if Meloni were to bring Futuro Nazionale into the right-wing coalition.

Spain: Cooling, Not Cracking
Skander Garchi Casal
(44 20) 7425 0034
Spain's economy should gradually cool, albeit still sustaining  robust 
momentum. The post-election policy environment  will be key to build 
conviction around our view.
A gradual deceleration ahead. Spain's economy should progressively cool over the next 
couple of years, but its elevated potential growth should still allow it to outperform the 
euro area over our forecast horizon. In 2026, GDP growth should remain broadly stable at 
2.6%Y ( Exhibit 131 ), before progressively decelerating to 2.1%Y in 2027 and 1.5%Y in 
2028. Two drivers remain center stage, underpinning our view: immigration and 
investment. We have previously argued that part of Spain's growth momentum has been 
supported by strong immigration and the EU's recovery fund programme (see here and 
here). Looking ahead, we see scope for both tailwinds to fade and eventually turn into 
headwinds in 2028. While the overall momentum should remain robust, a deceleration 
looks likely. We note, however, that uncertainty remains high ahead of the upcoming 
election, and greater clarity on the future policy mix would be needed to build more 
conviction around our view.
The immigration momentum is softening. Looking at the details, immigration inflows 
peaked in 2022 at almost 2.5% of the working-age population (WAP – Exhibit 129 ). Since 
then, they have gradually eased, stabilising at around 2% of WAP until mid-2025 before 
declining further to 1.6% and currently on a downward trend. While inflows remain strong 
by historical standards, it is notable that they have started to decelerate despite a 
supportive policy environment. More importantly, we see a risk that the policy backdrop 
becomes less supportive towards the back end of 2027, depending on the immigration 
policies pursued by the next government. The impact would ultimately depend on the 
policies implemented and the impact on  immigration flows, but we estimate that the 
associated drag on growth from lower net immigration could reach around 40bp, 
materialising with a lag in 2028.
A 20bp net drag from investment in 2028. When it comes to investments, the Recovery 
Fund should sustain robust momentum through 2027, with a large volume of grants 
expected to be received by end-2026 and spent during the year (+1pp of GDP). In 2028, 
however, the RRF impulse should fade. This should be partly offset by an acceleration in 
Cohesion Policy spending, leaving a net drag on GDP growth of only c.20bp. Together, 
these two channels explain the 60bp deceleration in growth we have pencilled for 2028.
More clarity around future policy should allow us to build more conviction. Our view 
remains subject to uncertainty, particularly towards the back end of our forecast  horizon, 
given the upcoming election and potential changes in the  policy landscape. We could start 
to gain more clarity by early 2Q27, as the 2027 budget is discussed and approved. We 
should therefore be able to build greater conviction as policy priorities of the next 
administration are revealed.

Norway: High Inflation, Prolonged Hold
Gabriela Silova
(49 69) 2166 2832
Inflation will remain high, but not rise further. Norges Bank remains on a 
prolonged hold until December 2027, when it starts to cut rates. 
Mainland up, offshore down. Consumer activity is due to pick up next year due to 
government support (tax breaks, Norgespris) and real wage growth, with a further boost 
in 2028 coming through monetary policy easing. The increase in energy commodity prices 
has been a tailwind for exports, offsetting the negative impact from the stronger currency, 
but production constraints limit the impact. Prospects for petroleum investment have the 
potential to improve, but in our base case we see offshore investments as a drag, due to 
the anticipated normalization after the 2022 tax break coupled with declining energy 
prices.  Private investments stand to benefit from AI data center build-out, but otherwise 
the recovery in the construction sector has been pushed out further to 2028. We note 
there is an upside to Norway's AI story (dependent on regulation), which could make the 
investment and import components grow, but have a limited impact on GDP owing to a 
large import share. 
Inflation | High but not higher. We expect inflation to move mostly sideways, but not 
accelerate further, over the next year, as lower goods and energy inflation compensates 
for expected robust services inflation. It should start to decline gradually in 3Q27. While 
we do not see inflation reaching the target over our forecast horizon, the stronger 
currency, softening pressures from energy prices through second-round effects, and lower 
wage demands should allow both headline and core inflation to fall towards 2.5%Y. We 
maintain our view that, absent external shocks, wages could decelerate in 2027 towards 
4.0% due to lower inflation forecasts, a slightly softer labor market and less favorable 
export sector profitability. Headline inflation remains shielded from higher electricity 
prices through Norgespris, despite an increase by  6.25 øre/kWh for 2027.  
Norges Bank | On hold until 4Q27. We see  Norges Bank remaining  on a prolonged hold at 
4.5%. Inflation is not expected to accelerate further and the Committee wants to avoid 
restricting the economy too much. Given a policy rate back at 4.5% and inflation forecasts 
pointing at roughly half of where inflation came in 2022/23, we think the response is at 
this stage sufficient. The Committee will likely keep the tightening bias, we believe, until 
the upside risks on the back of higher energy prices begin to subside and we get past the 
annual price resets and wage settlement. In line with our forecast for inflation starting to 
come in consistently around 2.5%Y and below from 4Q27, we keep our easing bias. We 
expect Norges Bank to deliver a first rate cut in December 2027, and then follow with two 
more cuts in June 2028 (after the wage settlement) and September 2028, down to 3.75%.  
Risks. Stronger demand would lead Norges Bank to tighten further, as would higher 
energy prices. In the latter scenario, Norges Bank cuts to a lower terminal rate due to the 
impact on demand. AI investment falling short of expectations would result in lower 
growth and Norges Bank cutting earlier and more.

Sweden: Golden Year Is Over, but Strength Remains
Gabriela Silova
(49 69) 2166 2832
Inflation path will be volatile.  Growth settles around potential as fiscal policy 
turns less supportive. We expect the Riksbank to hike to 2.25% in February. 
Less support, normalization ahead. Consumer demand and public investment 
significantly benefitted from the  2026 Budget bill and subsequent targeted measures in 
response to elevated energy prices. Going forward, we expect economic activity to remain 
strong and the output gap to close, but the momentum to decelerate. With government 
formation in deadlock and temporary measures being phased out, we continue to expect 
fiscal policy to become gradually less supportive. The exception is defense spending, 
which remains financed by additional borrowing until 2034. Higher long-term yields will 
slow the pick-up in private investments, we believe, while less supportive monetary policy 
will impact disposable income of households.  AI data center build-out is positively 
impacting private investment and industrial growth in computer and electronics, but the 
net impact on growth remains limited owing to a large share of spending on imports.  
Overall, we expect growth momentum to slow down but remain close to its potential.  
Inflation |  Volatility ahead. Inflation in Sweden remains at record low levels due to 
temporary measures (food VAT, public transport ticket, fuel VAT cuts) and weaker 
momentum, despite high energy prices. Still, we think the only way from here is up. Base 
effects and reversals could lead to significant jumps and volatility  in the next two years. 
Indicators of underlying inflationary pressures are strengthening on the back of higher 
energy prices, and should support the return of core inflation towards the target. The 
realized krona weakening  will contribute to higher inflation in 2027, but reverse its impact 
in 2028. The weak labor market will play a dampening role in the upcoming two-year 
wage settlement. We expect a settlement within the vicinity of 6.1% across two years. We 
see headline inflation peaking in 2Q27, and base effects on energy dampening the impact 
of food VAT reversal on headline in January 2028.  
Riksbank |  Normalization ahead. We expect the Riksbank to deliver one more rate hike to 
2.25%, bringing the policy rate into the mid-point of neutral rate estimates. A gradual 
approach is favored by the Board. We  continue to expect the second rate hike in February 
2027, as long as energy prices stays elevated and growth remains  resilient.  For the 
remainder of the forecast period, we expect the Riksbank to remain in neutral territory. 
We see core inflation (excluding temporary fiscal measures) staying around target in 
2028, and economic activity settling around potential. 
Risks. Our global risk scenarios have implications for Sweden. Stronger demand could 
cause the Riksbank to tighten further. Higher energy prices and yields could also lead to 
more rate hikes, but ultimately result in lower terminal rate due to the impact on demand. 
AI investment falling short of expectations could result in lower growth and the Riksbank 
remaining on hold before easing again. More pronounced AI productivity boost could lead 
to stronger growth but less inflation, resulting in an unchanged policy path.

Canada: Animal Spirits Take Hold
Arunima Sinha
+1 212 761-4125
Mayank Phadke
+1 212 761-1531
Growth strengthens in 2027 and 2028, with  business investment unlocking the 
stronger productivity scenario discussed in Canada: The Next Acceleration, 
while consumption remains resilient. The output gap closes, allowing greater 
energy pass-through to core alongside other inflationary pressures, leading the 
BoC to tighten policy to 3.5% by end-2027. 
Canada's recovery increasingly rests on business investment, as policy support and 
improving intentions translate into spending. We see growth strengthening through 
2027 and running above 2% through 2028, absorbing excess capacity. Investment is a 
necessary step toward the stronger productivity growth we have outlined: rebuilding 
capital per worker must precede sustained efficiency gains, which build gradually. 
Consumption remains resilient but provides limited acceleration. Real spending growth 
settles near 2% through 2028, constrained by moderate real labor-income gains as 
immigration caps slow labor-force growth and inflation absorbs much of nominal wage 
growth in 2027. Spending already running ahead of labor income limits further upside. 
Non-residential investment strengthens from 2.1% in 2026 to 3.7% in 2027 and 4.5% in 
2028, underpinned by above-average investment intentions, gradual support from the 
Productivity Mega-Deduction and diminishing uncertainty. Exports recover from tariff-
affected levels, but net trade weighs on growth. New capacity and diversification take 
time, while tariff-affected sectors remain weak. Business adapt to uncertainty and costs, 
but this does not replace lost US demand. 
Stronger growth absorbs the excess capacity that had kept inflation pass-through 
contained. Headline inflation stays near 3.2%Y through 1Q27 on energy; but energy costs 
increasingly reach core goods and selected services, while food and electronics contribute 
more as energy fades. Shelter rebuilds modestly, but labor costs are not a driver. Inflation 
peaks in 1H27, and headline returns to 2% in 2H28, with core around 2.1%. 
We expect quarterly 25bp BoC hikes to 3.5% through 2027, then a hold in 2028, 
possibly with an upward revision to the BoC's neutral estimate. Broader pass-through and 
diminishing slack require tightening beyond the initial insurance moves. Risks lean toward 
the downside. Our baseline relies on investment intentions becoming spending, and 
uncertainty hurts intentions more than upside scenarios boost them. Stronger US demand 
could bring investment forward; higher productivity would support demand with less 
inflation. Higher rates and oil prices could delay capex and weaken consumption, while an 
AI correction would affect Canada only indirectly. Tariff re-escalation or an adverse 
USMCA outcome could undermine investment, especially after tightening.

Australia: Broadening Weakness
Chris Read
(61 2) 9770 1513
Core inflation stays sticky into mid 2027, keeping the RBA at its 4.85% peak 
after the November hike. Growth slows as higher rates, falling house prices and 
weak real incomes weigh on consumers, and unemployment rises above 5%. We 
expect that slack to allow two cuts in 2H27 and further easing in 2028.
Growth slows from a firm 2026. We expect GDP growth of 2.1% in 2026, before slowing 
to 1.3% in 2027 as the policy tightening works through. That is below consensus and the 
RBA's November forecasts. Tighter policy is the key driver, with the RBA having raised the 
cash rate five times this year.
Housing and the consumer carry the adjustment. National house prices are already well 
below their March peak, and we expect a 10-15% peak-to-trough fall, with a stabilization 
(but not a recovery) through 2H27. Weaker real incomes as headline inflation peaks in 
4Q26, and wealth headwinds from the housing correction, see consumption growth slow 
to 1.1% in 2027. Dwelling investment also follows house prices lower through 2027 and 
2028.
Capex and public demand cushion the slowdown. Business investment remains a partial 
offset, rising almost 10% in 2026 on a structural pipeline (data centers, defense and 
energy infrastructure) that is relatively insensitive to interest rates, while outside these 
areas a weaker domestic backdrop contributes to some slowing. Public demand also 
continues to grow steadily, but fiscal constraints may mean the tailwind is not as large as 
has been seen over the past few years.
Labor market continues loosening. We expect employment growth to slow, lifting the 
unemployment rate to 5.2% by end-2027 and 5.4% by end-2028, above pre-Covid levels 
and our estimates of full employment. Wage growth remain somewhat sticky and weak 
productivity growth holds unit labor cost growth elevated, both of which limit the help 
the labor market gives the inflation path.
RBA on hold, then cuts as slack builds. We expect headline CPI to peak  in 4Q26 on high 
fuel prices before falling back sharply through 2027. Underlying inflation is slower to ease. 
We expect the trimmed mean to stay above 3%Y into mid 2027 and to reduce only 
gradually after that, broadly in line with the RBA's November forecasts. The RBA raised 
the cash rate to 4.85% in November and we expect it to hold through 1H27.  We think there 
will be sufficient evidence of slack by the second half of the year, and expect cuts in 
August and November 2027, with further easing in 2028 to 3.85%.
The main risk is inflation. If energy prices stay near current levels, or the trimmed mean 
keeps rising at a faster pace into the June quarter, the RBA is likely to remain at its peak 
through 2027 and a further hike becomes a risk. The main downside risk is housing. If 
house prices keep falling at the recent pace, the peak-to-trough fall would be materially 
larger, which would weaken consumption further and bring the cuts forward.

continue to grind higher through 2027 and 2028

China: Lowflation Lingers, New Hope Takes Shape
Robin Xing
(852) 2848 6511
China Economics Team  
China remains in a prolonged economic transition. We expect real GDP growth 
to moderate in 2027. Robust exports leave room for continued fiscal 
consolidation, while household deleveraging caps consumption, pushing 
nominal GDP growth below 5%Y. From 2028, easing drags from balance sheet 
stress and broader tech diffusion should support macro stabilization.
Exports cushion weaker domestic demand. We see real GDP growth easing to 4.4%Y in 
2027 from 4.6%Y in 2026. The mix remains uneven. Household spending should stay 
subdued amid weak employment and income expectations, while government 
consumption slows on increased financing pressures. Infrastructure investment may pick 
up modestly with quasi-fiscal support, cushioning domestic demand, which will likely slow 
to 3.1%Y from 3.5%Y. Export volume growth moderates but stays solid, while weaker 
import demand lifts net exports' growth contribution to 1.5pp from 1.3pp. Spillovers to 
domestic demand remain limited by overcapacity and weak private-sector sentiment.
Back to lowflation. This year's rise in headline inflation, particularly PPI, has been driven 
mainly by energy and key upstream commodities amid supply disruptions, alongside 
chipflation. However, excess capacity and weak household demand constrain pricing 
power, while subdued wage growth and fiscal consolidation put a damper on reflation. In 
turn, fading energy price shock and supply response should give way to lowflation in 2027. 
Higher food inflation due to El Niño could keep headline CPI broadly steady at a subpar 
level, while core inflation and PPI should soften. Consequently, the GDP deflator should 
moderate, leaving nominal growth to slow by 1pp to 4.8%.
Fiscal consolidation continues through the debt-swap cliff. We expect the augmented 
deficit to narrow to 10.5% of GDP in 2027 from 11% in 2026, following a 1.4pp contraction 
in 2026 on slow spending. The key drag is the Rmb2tn step-down in annual debt-swap 
support tightens local cash flows, weighing on public consumption. Stricter tax and social 
security contribution collection, falling land-sale revenues and restraint on local leverage 
sustain the fiscal drag. Funds carried over from 2026 will likely provide a partial offset. 
Spending stays supply-centric, favoring infrastructure and industrial upgrading through 
special treasury issuance and policy-bank support, while consumer trade-in subsidies 
taper. We expect the PBoC to keep policy rates broadly unchanged, providing stable 
liquidity and targeted credit support for technology upgrading and strategic infrastructure.
Tech gains start to broaden into the macro economy from 2028. After 2-3 years of 
strong exports, solid tech progress but a muted macro response, we think the picture 
could start to change from 2028. Robust global industrial demand, greater supply 
discipline and wider AI adoption should lift capacity utilization, productivity and corporate 
earnings. Alongside fading drags from housing and local debt, these gains should gradually 
support employment, incomes and domestic demand. We see real GDP growth stabilizing 
and inflation recovering gradually, creating room for modest fiscal consolidation.

RMB gains face a speed limit. We expect renminbi appreciation against the dollar to slow 
in 2027, with USD/CNY reaching 6.60 at year-end from 6.65 at end-2026, before declining 
to 6.45 at end-2028. A large current account surplus and sustained exporter FX 
conversion should support the currency. However, subdued domestic demand and low 
inflation may limit Beijing's tolerance for another year of rapid appreciation, particularly 
against a stronger dollar. We see the PBoC leaning against one-way expectations through 
fixing guidance and measures to encourage more balanced cross-border flows, such as 
verbal guidance, macro-prudential parameters governing cross-border financing, and 
outbound investment quotas. In 2028, improving fundamentals and domestic asset 
returns should support modestly faster appreciation.
Risks to watch. In a bull scenario, stronger global demand, additional local debt relief, and 
a shift toward social welfare support strengthen domestic demand. In a bear scenario, an 
AI investment slowdown weakens exports and deflates global asset prices, while deeper 
property weakness further strains local finances and private consumption. Meanwhile, the 
policy response remains focused on industrial and infrastructure investment rather than 
household support, worsening the supply-demand imbalance and reigniting severe 
deflation.

India: Growth Near Trend; External Risks in Focus
Upasana Chachra
(91 22) 618 2246  
Bani Gambhir  
(91 22) 618 3027 
We expect India's momentum to remain resilient as growth normalizes towards 
trend, supported by cyclical pick-up in domestic and external demand. Macro 
stability risks remain in check, supported by prudent and calibrated fiscal and 
monetary policy. Risks to outlook are balanced. 
Growth – normalization amid resilience. India's growth has surprised on the upside this 
year, with activity holding up better than expected despite external headwinds, including 
global trade uncertainty and adverse terms of trade amid geopolitical tensions. High-
frequency data remain robust, particularly for capex-related indicators. In CY27, we expect 
GDP growth to normalize to 7% YoY from a high base, driven by slower rural demand on 
account of adverse weather, a modest uptick in real rates, and higher inflation weighing on 
growth. However, the turnaround in capex drivers provides a foundation for a durable 
recovery, and we expect fiscal and monetary policy to remain calibrated and prudent.
Consumption – urban to outperform rural demand. Expanding capex should aid job 
growth, alongside nascent signs of a pick-up in income growth. Urban demand remains 
strong, supported by resilient auto sales and healthy festive spending. We remain cautious 
on rural demand, given weaker farm incomes from adverse weather and terms of trade, 
though last year's strong farm incomes and welfare safeguards should cushion the impact. 
Higher inflation and softer consumer confidence are risks to discretionary spending.
Investment – India is in the middle of a sustained, increasingly broad-based capex 
upcycle, with public spending  complemented by a gradual revival in private capital 
expenditure. We expect investment to remain firm, supported by healthy corporate and 
bank balance sheets, sustained public infrastructure spending and policy incentives. 
Structural growth is driven by four areas: energy transition and security, defense, 
industrial onshoring  and infrastructure, and global supply-chain diversification. Higher 
borrowing costs and global uncertainty could weigh on near-term decisions, but improving 
demand visibility and capacity utilization should support broader private capex.
Trade –  diversification and currency competitiveness to support exports. India's goods 
and services exports account for about one-fifth of GDP and remain an important 
contributor to growth. Goods exports have been strong, particularly non-oil, led by 
electronics, engineering goods, autos and chemicals. Services exports have grown at a 
robust 9.8% year to date, albeit lower than their peak levels. Going forward, goods 
exports should benefit from new FTAs, a competitive currency and the government's push 
on manufacturing competitiveness. On services, we remain watchful of AI's impact on 
software exports, even as we expect GCC exports to keep expanding.
Price stability –  broadening price pressures. We expect headline CPI  to accelerate 
towards 5.2% in 2027, driven by higher food inflation amid weaker monsoon conditions, 
alongside potential spillovers from elevated input costs. Core CPI should remain

contained at ~4%, suggesting manageable demand-side pressures. However, persistent 
food inflation could weigh on purchasing power and sentiment, while rising inflation 
expectations raise the risk of second-round effects. We expect headline CPI to moderate in 
2028 on a normal monsoon and easing food prices, though the  trajectory is vulnerable to 
weather shocks, commodity price volatility and spillovers to underlying inflation. 
External stability –  navigating headwinds; buffers to support external balance sheet 
strength. We expect a benign current account deficit at around 0.8% of GDP in 2027-28, 
in line with the  post-pandemic average, aided by more favorable terms of trade from 
lower global commodity prices, and range-bound services exports and remittances. Capital 
flows are key, having weighed on the currency for two years despite a strong external 
balance sheet, which should be supported by a neutral to mildly surplus BoP in CY27. 
Adequate reserves, comfortable import cover and low external debt should cushion 
against commodity price volatility, capital flow fluctuations and shifts in risk sentiment.
Policy – prudent normalization. We anticipate three hikes, taking the terminal policy rate 
to 6.25%. This represents a measured normalization of real policy rates, which we forecast 
to average 1% in CY27 vs 0.8% in CY26, keeping them close to neutral. The move aims to 
contain second-round inflationary pressures and anchor inflation expectations, with 
limited downside risks to growth. The RBI is likely, we think, to remain data-dependent, 
calibrating its response across rates, liquidity and regulatory measures.
On the fiscal policy front, we expect the government to maintain fiscal prudence while 
prioritising growth-enhancing capital expenditure. In CY27, we expect mild fiscal 
consolidation as capex is prioritized, while creating room for higher salaries and pensions 
following the Pay Commission. This remains aligned with the medium-term target of 
bringing down central government debt to 50% of GDP by 2031.
Risks – broadly balanced. Upside risks include stronger domestic demand, a smaller-than-
anticipated impact from weak agriculture and faster private capex recovery. Downside 
risks include weaker global growth, escalating geopolitical tensions, prolonged tariff 
uncertainty, potential US policy changes and AI-led disruption to services exports. 
Persistent inflation and tighter financial conditions could further weigh on consumption 
and investment.

Indonesia: Lack of Policy Space
Derrick Kam 
(65) 6834 8272 
Currency stability constrains monetary easing. Fuel subsidies and the deficit 
ceiling limit fiscal flexibility.
Lingering uncertainty and limited policy space. While growth outturns have been better 
than expected, we believe lingering uncertainty, which in turn has kept financial conditions 
tight, will lead to a growth moderation in 2027. A combination of limited monetary and 
fiscal space may mean that the policy environment is not conducive to an easing of 
financial conditions. 
Monetary policy remains focused on the rupiah. The BI held its policy rate at 5.75% in 
October, following the earlier tightening to stabilize the rupiah. We expect policymakers 
to balance exchange rate stability against the drag from higher borrowing costs on 
domestic demand. Hedging incentives provide an alternative tool to attract capital inflows, 
which may help to stabilize the currency. But we continue to view the risks of renewed 
tightening as high. 
Fiscal space remains limited. The approved 2027 budget targets a deficit of 2.4% of GDP, 
preserving policymakers' commitment to the 3% ceiling. However, this limits the room for 
broad countercyclical support, especially given the backdrop of a continued decline in 
revenue/GDP ratios. We assume subsidized fuel prices remain unchanged; however, with 
international oil prices still higher than what was assumed in the budget, the subsidy bill 
may be larger, which would then act as a potential restraint on spending elsewhere. 
Public investment could support capex. We have previously identified public investment 
projects as a way to kick-start a sustained capex cycle, as we believe that this would help 
to anchor private-sector confidence, pulling in private investment alongside public 
investment. 
Meanwhile, stronger commodity prices and a pick-up in pace of reforms could result in 
a more constructive outlook. Given Indonesia is a commodity exporter, an improvement 
in terms of trade would lend support to the currency and financial conditions. A sustained 
improvement in coal, palm oil and nickel prices would strengthen export incomes and 
terms of trade. Typically, an improvement in terms of trade also lifts capex, especially 
among commodity exporters. Higher export receipts would support the rupiah, easing 
financial conditions and encouraging investment among non-commodity producers too. A 
pick-up in the pace of policy reforms could boost corporate and investor sentiment and 
provide a more conducive environment for capital to be put to work.
Risks. Faster reform and effective public capex offer upside. Renewed rupiah weakness 
would further constrain policy room. Prolonged high oil prices could squeeze purchasing 
power and delay a broader investment recovery.

Korea: Spillover Cycle – Capex Deepens, Services Support
Kathleen Oh
(852) 2848 7340
Korea's macro resilience is set to endure in 2027 as robust exports  broaden into 
domestic accounts. An unprecedented tech windfall should translate into 
accelerated capex, stronger income and larger fiscal spending. With sticky, 
above-target inflation lingering, we look for the BoK to hike to 3.50% by 1Q27, 
then pause until 2H28.
Aggregate macro resilience to sustain. Korea's macro resilience should carry through 
2027 as robust external demand increasingly broadens into the domestic economy, in our 
view. An unprecedented tech windfall is set to drive faster domestic capex activity, higher 
household income, and significant fiscal spending. As a result, we see another year of 
above-potential growth of 3.0% in 2027 after a strong rebound of 3.8% in 2026, before 
moderating to 2.4% in 2028 on base effects and tech momentum normalizing.
Capex deepening and services supported. We expect positive domestic spillovers to 
anchor growth in 2027. Following an exceptional, tech-driven GDP expansion in 2026, 
domestic demand will take the lead as accelerating facilities investment and nominal wage 
gains lift consumption, offsetting waning net export contributions. Although 
semiconductor exports still benefit from AI-driven memory shortages, annual momentum 
will likely normalize on high base. We also flag that domestic sectors isolated from tech 
momentum face persistent fragility beneath this headline resilience.
Sticky, slow-burn inflation remaining above target. Factoring in sustained energy price 
headwinds and a broadening domestic activity rebound, we maintain our above-target 
inflation forecasts at 2.5% for 2027 and 2.1% for 2028, down from 2.7% in 2026. On core 
CPI, we see an even stronger trend at 2.6% and 2.2%, respectively, following the initial 
absorption of energy costs and inflation exhibiting second-round stickiness. With resilient 
demand-pull forces and sticky services prices, we see inflation only moderating gradually 
toward the target in 2H28.
Monetary and fiscal policy settings. We maintain our view of the terminal rate reaching 
3.50% in 1Q27 and pausing through 2028.  Amid less urgent concerns on the financial 
stability front, and KRW strength coupled with constrained household credit growth, we 
see the BoK prioritizing bringing inflation to target. Backed by strong confidence in 
growth, we see the BoK adopting a higher-for-longer policy stance before gradually easing 
in 2H28. On the fiscal side, ample capacity should support growth amid a 30% rise in 
anticipated tax revenue for 2027, a consolidated fiscal surplus facilitates expansionary 
policy, which should lift GDP growth by roughly 50 bps.
Risk balance tilts to the downside amid increased risks to a delayed global AI capex cycle 
and investment disruption. This could  delay domestic investment and construction 
activities for  data center builds and energy supply infrastructure.

Taiwan: The Biggest Beneficiary of AI
Kathleen Oh
(852) 2848 7340
Taiwan's economy is one of the biggest beneficiaries of AI supercycle. After 
decade-high export-driven growth in 2026, we think it is set for another year of 
resilient expansion on AI. Amid a wider broadening-out effect on the domestic 
economy, we expect above-target inflation and the CBC to hike to 2.25%. 
Broadening from exports to capex. We remain constructive on Taiwan's macro outlook 
heading into 2027. Following an upward revision of our 2026 GDP growth forecast to 
13.5% (vs 11.5% previously), we project another robust expansion of 8.7% in 2027 (vs 7.6%) 
before moderating to 6.1% in 2028. Resilient real exports driven by AI hardware shipments 
continue to reinforce domestic capex while progressively boosting household incomes. 
Because export cycles historically lead capex by roughly two quarters, buoyant 2026 tech 
order flows will likely to sustain machinery and fab expansion well into 1H27, anchoring 
full-year fixed capex growth at 17.5%Y before normalizing in 2H27.
Consumption cushion is key. We forecast private consumption growth to accelerate from 
5.3%Y in 2026 to 7.7% in 2027, cushioning aggregate domestic activity as real export 
volume gains normalize. Household spending expanded broadly throughout 2026, 
underpinned by communications, recreation, transport, outbound tourism, and retail trade 
amid positive wealth effects and real income improvements. Although wage gains remain 
skewed toward AI supply chains relative to domestic services, resilient employment 
sentiment should sustain spending into 1H27, bolstered by the government's second round 
of cash handouts starting in January 2027.
Sticky inflation facing demand-side pressure. We expect inflation to stay moderately 
above the 2.0% target in 2027-28 after rising sharply in 2026. Core inflation (excluding 
vegetables, fruit and energy) will outpace headline, particularly in services, as domestic 
spending benefits from positive wealth effects and fiscal support amid softening oil prices. 
The AI boom and record capex should keep core inflation elevated in 2027 before lower 
global oil prices ease headline pressures toward 2028. Lagging services and rent costs 
should hold headline and core above target at 2.1% and 2.2%, respectively, in 2027.
CBC to engage in a mild hiking cycle to 2.25%. With the CBC viewing inflation around 2% 
as an acceptable threshold, we expect a cautious tightening path through 2027 to rein in 
persistent price pressures. Following an initial hike to 2.125% in December 2026 driven by 
resilient, growth-led inflation, we project a move to 2.25% in June 2027 to curb upward 
pressures stemming from fiscal stimulus and broader domestic demand spillovers from 
tech exports. We anticipate the CBC will begin gradual monetary easing only toward late 
2028 as growth moderates following two years of exceptional AI-driven expansion.
Risk balance tilts to the downside amid a slow broadening-out effect from exports to 
consumption on muted wage growth; potential delay to global AI capex cycle could 
disrupt  domestic investment growth.

95
Thailand: Positive Policy Setup for Reflation
Derrick Kam 
(65) 6834 8272 
We see a continuation of accommodative monetary and fiscal policy as setting 
up a positive backdrop for reflating the economy. A sustained momentum in 
investment will be key to kick-start a virtuous cycle. 
Green shoots are emerging. We see a constructive macro backdrop heading into 2027. 
Exports are improving alongside stronger foreign direct investment. Policy remains 
supportive. This improves the prospects for recovery following a period of weak domestic 
demand. Investment commitments will need to result in construction and equipment 
purchases that create jobs before the recovery spreads more widely across the economy. 
Deleveraging is becoming less of a cyclical drag. Household debt is often perceived to be 
a structural constraint. However, household debt-to-GDP ratios have already declined 
from their peak, and this should gradually ease their drag on activity. Moreover, despite 
the pressures on cost of living, fiscal policy measures have kicked in to prevent the 
erosion of household purchasing power from adversely affecting consumption. 
Monetary policy stays lower for longer. We think the BoT is likely to maintain an 
accommodative stance. We expect the central bank to look past inflation generated by 
higher energy costs, since rate hikes would not address the supply shock. Low nominal 
rates would also help the recovery if nominal incomes strengthened. An improvement in 
income growth relative to financing costs would support household finances and business 
investment. 
Fiscal execution remains important. Coordinated monetary and fiscal support remains 
central to lifting Thailand out of its low-growth, low-inflation loop. The extension of the 
consumption co-payment programme supports demand. A durable recovery will also 
require spending beyond temporary transfers. 
Investment can strengthen potential growth. Indeed, policymakers have been working 
on enabling this transition from supporting consumption in the near term to improving 
investment over the medium term. Improving FDI trends offer a route to stronger 
productivity and export competitiveness. The Thailand Fast Pass initiative aims to shorten 
approval timelines for major projects, while the OECD accession process provides an 
external framework for enacting structural reforms intended to ease the cost of doing 
business and ultimately bring about stronger investment, as well as supporting Thailand's 
reintegration into global supply chains. 
Risks center on execution and credit transmission. Delays in public spending or 
investment approvals could leave the recovery concentrated in export industries. Credit 
transmission is uneven, as SME lending continues to contract despite stronger lending to 
large companies. Higher energy prices would weaken purchasing power and put further 
pressure on borrowers. On the flipside, faster project execution and firmer household 
incomes would allow the recovery to spread more quickly into domestic demand.

96
Malaysia: Patient Policy
Derrick Kam 
(65) 6834 8272 
Improving competitiveness and sustained investment should keep growth 
robust. BNM remains patient, but the recovery supports the case for eventual 
monetary normalization.
A strong starting point. The Malaysian economy has continued to benefit from the 
electronics cycle and sustained inbound investment. Gains in market share of exports and 
FDI suggest that the country is strengthening its position within global supply chains, 
providing support beyond the current technology upswing.
Investment remains central. Manufacturing investment, including electronics, and digital 
infrastructure are reinforcing demand and expanding productive capacity. The 13th 
Malaysia Plan and New Industrial Master Plan 2030 provides a policy framework for 
industrial upgrading. The key question is how quickly investment commitments will 
translate into spending on construction and equipment, and ultimately into production. As 
projects proceed, stronger domestic supplier linkages should improve employment 
opportunities and spread the benefits of stronger external demand. Malaysia should also 
benefit as Asia's industrial and capex cycle extends beyond AI. Private consumption should 
remain an important pillar of the expansion, supported by employment and income 
growth.  
Inflationary pressures to stay contained. Inflationary pressures have built up gradually 
over the course of 2026, but are still contained. One key factor has been that domestic 
fuel prices remain unchanged. For 2027, we see largely a repeat, where growth stays 
relatively robust, driven by exports and continued capacity expansion, which should help 
to keep inflationary pressures contained. 
BNM remains patient. The central bank maintained its policy rate at 2.75% in November, 
extending the pause. The rate increase we had expected earlier in 2026 has yet to 
materialize. However, we continue to see a case for reversing the earlier insurance cut as 
growth remains robust and the recovery broadens. 
Fiscal consolidation continues. Budget 2027 targets a deficit of 3.3% of GDP, compared 
with the revised 3.6% estimate for 2026. This keeps fiscal policy on a consolidation path 
while preserving support for households and development spending. For growth, the 
composition and execution of expenditure will matter alongside the headline deficit. 
Public investment that facilitates private projects can reinforce the capex cycle. However, 
the subsidy burden remains sensitive to international energy prices, potentially 
constraining the resources available for other priorities. 
Risks. A sharper slowdown in global electronics demand would weaken exports and 
investment. Prolonged energy pressures could complicate fiscal consolidation. The 
timeline for elections is also an important risk factor to monitor. On the upside, faster 
project execution and a broader export recovery could sustain stronger growth for longer.

97
Philippines: Stagflationary Conditions Persist
Derrick Kam 
(65) 6834 8272 
Headline inflation has stayed much higher than GDP growth, leading 
stagflationary conditions to persist. This backdrop also constrains policy 
support and, with inflation expectations still unanchored, we see the scope for 
further rate hikes. 
The stagflationary backdrop has persisted. The economy enters 2027 from a weaker 
starting point than we had expected. GDP growth slowed further to 2.1%Y in 3Q26 – the 
lowest since the Global Financial Crisis (aside from the Covid shock), while October 
headline inflation moved higher to 7.8%Y. 
Domestic growth drivers remain weak. Household consumption grew by only 2.4%Y in 
3Q26 and capital formation contracted by 0.2%. We continue to expect a difficult 
recovery. Higher living costs are restraining real incomes, weakening household purchasing 
power. The contraction in investment suggests that corporate confidence remains too 
subdued, while tighter monetary conditions add to borrowing costs, which may limit the 
improvement in investment that will be needed to bring about a stronger recovery. 
Inflation remains the binding policy constraint. The Philippines has high sensitivity to 
food and energy costs. We expect a full pass-through of higher international oil prices to 
domestic fuel prices. Elevated core inflation points to price pressures extending beyond 
the initial energy price shock. Moreover, El Niño presents risks to food inflation. 
BSP must prioritize inflation expectations. The central bank raised rates by 50bp on 
November 9, taking the policy rate to 5.50%. We continue to see a policy bias toward 
guarding against second-round effects, despite weak growth. Higher core inflation 
increases the importance of anchoring inflation expectations, which we think will have to 
be met two more rate hikes. The recent decline in oil prices provides welcome relief, but 
we believe that headline inflation will need to move below the 2-4% target range before 
monetary policy can pivot to provide meaningful growth support. 
Fiscal delivery becomes more important. The proposed 2027 budget includes increases 
in capital outlays, creating scope for stronger public investment, within the constraints of 
limited fiscal space. The pace of spending depends on projects moving through approval 
and procurement to construction. Faster disbursements would support capital formation, 
helping to offset private-sector weakness. 
Downside risks still dominate. Another renewed rise in oil prices would raise energy 
import costs and lead to further unanchoring of inflation expectations, which may require 
more rate hikes than we have in our base case. Similarly, more persistent food inflation 
than expected would delay the restoration of real incomes and keep monetary conditions 
tight. We remain cautious about growth returning toward its previous trend, given that 
investment-to-GDP ratios remain well below their previous trends, while the services 
exports segment stays under pressure given the spectre of AI adoption.
