Hong Kong: A Hiccup, Not a Stall
 Jenny Zheng 
(852) 3963 4015 
Hong Kong will likely resume above-trend growth from 2Q27 after a hiccup 
near-term amid Fed rate hikes. A shorter and shallower Fed hike cycle, stronger 
bank funding, and continued talent inflows would ensure a continued housing 
upcycle, while Asia capex cycle supports re-exports. 
Renewed Fed hikes should cause a modest growth setback in late 2026/early 2027, we 
believe. We expect Hong Kong's real GDP growth to slow to 1.7% QoQ SAAR in 4Q26-
1Q27 from an average 5.7% in 1Q-3Q26. Under the Linked Exchange Rate Regime (LERS), 
higher rates should curb capex, delay home purchases and weaken consumption through 
negative wealth effects from lower asset prices.
Growth should rebound to above-trend from 2Q27, however, with scope for further 
strengthening in 2028. We forecast 3.2% growth in 2027 and 4.5% in 2028 (consensus: 
2.9%/2.6%; pre-Covid trend: 2.8%), supported by:
• A smaller Fed shock and stronger liquidity. We expect 75bp cumulative Fed hikes, 
ending by March 2027, versus 525bp in the last cycle. Banks' HKD loan-to-deposit 
ratio, near post-2009 lows at ~70%, should limit local rate increases to ~50bp in 1-
month HIBOR and 12.5bp in HSBC's prime rate. Clarity on peak financing costs 
should anchor confidence and encourage deferred purchases and investment.
• Firmer housing support. Our Hong Kong property team expects another 5% rise in 
residential prices in 2027 after a stall over the next one to two quarters. Tight 
supply and talent immigration should outweigh modestly higher mortgage rates, 
and planned hedge fund carried interest tax concessions, once approved, should 
attract more talent inflows from other financial centers, adding to housing 
purchase/rental demand.
• Asia's capex cycle supports re-exports. We expect investment across Asia to 
withstand higher rates and oil prices, supported by non-restrictive financing costs  
and healthy private balance sheets in most economies. Continued strength in 
cross-border equipment and component demand should benefit Hong Kong's re-
export business. 
Risks. An upside scenario could include a more dovish Fed stance and Beijing's more 
decisive economic rebalancing towards consumption. This would ease the upward 
pressures on HIBOR and attract more global capital into HK-listed Chinese equities, 
allowing for stronger capital and property market  wealth effect  on consumption. 
Downside risks include more aggressive Fed tightening, more aggressive pro-cyclical 
tightening in China, and stricter outbound investment controls, amplifying HIBOR repricing 
and asset-price weakness.

99
Singapore: Growth Moderates but Broadens
Derrick Kam 
(65) 6834 8272 
Asia's industrial and capex cycle should sustain growth beyond manufacturing. 
We expect MAS to hold in January, with April tightening possible if inflation 
pressures build.
Growth has been clocking well above trend. The Singapore economy has continued a 
much higher-than-trend growth rate in 2026, supported by the upswing in electronics and 
tech-related trade. After posting the third consecutive year of 5% GDP growth or more, 
we expect a moderation in headline GDP growth but see the recovery broadening out. 
Stronger exports should lift business investment, with improving employment supporting 
household spending. 
The industrial cycle remains supportive. Singapore benefits from the industrial and 
capex cycle across Asia. Electronics and precision engineering have led the expansion. 
Demand for trade and logistics services should rise with investment across the region. 
Financial and business services should also benefit as companies invest across the region. 
We expect stronger demand for these services to support domestic employment. We see 
the recovery in non-tech exports reaching more sectors during 2027. 
Underlying inflation dynamics warrant continued attention. The MAS expects core 
inflation of 1.5-2.0% in 2026 and 1.5-2.5% in 2027. We will watch whether higher energy 
costs pass through to a broader range of prices. So far, domestic price pressures are 
relatively subdued, even against a backdrop of robust growth. The lack of pass-through 
reflects two issues. First, part of the robust growth has been relatively narrow, which has 
limited the spillovers. Second, the supply side of the economy has proven relatively 
flexible, which has helped in containing domestic price pressures. We will be monitoring 
services inflation and labor costs to track whether the broader base of economic activity 
that we anticipate will generate domestic price pressures. 
MAS likely to hold in January. After increasing the appreciation slope very slightly in July, 
the MAS maintained its policy settings in October. We expect a hold in January 2027, as 
incoming data are unlikely to materially change its assessment of moderating growth and 
subsequently easing inflation. Tightening could resume at the April review if inflation 
pressures build. That risk would increase if the expansion generated more persistent 
domestic price pressure than the MAS currently anticipates. The pace at which growth 
spreads beyond manufacturing will therefore remain relevant. 
Risks remain external, with a high beta to global growth dynamics. A weaker 
semiconductor cycle or renewed supply disruptions would weigh on trade and growth. 
Stronger non-tech exports and faster regional investment could extend the expansion, 
while raising the likelihood that the MAS needs to tighten further. Given the starting point 
of robust growth and Singapore's high trade orientation and exposure to electronics, we 
believe the risks to the outlook are skewed towards the downside.

Saudi Arabia: Growth Regains Ground
Yomna Moheieldin
(44 20) 7677 0771
We expect Saudi GDP growth to rebound strongly in 2027 as oil activity 
normalizes, following a contraction in 2026 as lower oil export volumes weigh 
on growth. Resilient private consumption should continue to support non-oil 
activity, despite tighter financial conditions and investment headwinds, while 
higher oil revenues should provide some relief to domestic liquidity.
Growth to rebound strongly in 2027, with oil normalization the key driver. We expect a 
recovery in 4Q26 oil production, with 2026 real GDP contracting by 1.6%Y, before a 
strong oil-led rebound in 2027 leading to headline growth of 6.4%Y. We continue to 
assume export volumes normalize in 2Q27, with oil GDP contracting by 19% in 2026 
before expanding by around 23% in 2027 as production recovers.
Non-oil resilience to continue supporting headline growth recovery, although 
investment faces rising headwinds. Non-oil growth accelerated to 1.9%Y in 3Q26 from 
0.9%Y in 2Q26, in line with our thesis of resilient non-oil activities. 4Q26 consumer 
spending indicators remain constructive, despite an acceleration in attacks on both oil and 
non-oil infrastructure  while the recovery in PMI points to continued private-sector 
expansion. However, higher logistics, transportation and input costs are weighing on 
corporate margins, while tight domestic liquidity and higher global rates are raising 
funding costs and weighing on credit growth. We expect these pressures to constrain 
investments and loan growth in 2027,  even as resilient consumption continues to support 
non-oil activity. However, the normalization of oil exports and oil prices remaining above 
pre-conflict levels should provide some relief to domestic liquidity as higher oil revenues 
support government deposits in the banking system.
Fiscal buffers hold, but expenditure becomes more selective. Our buffers thesis 
continues to play out, with higher oil prices cushioning the impact of constrained 
production and exports on government revenues. We expect fiscal revenues to rise by 
around 6%Y in 2026 and a further 10%Y in 2027 as oil exports recover. Expenditure 
continues to exceed revenue, keeping the fiscal accounts in deficit at 5.6% of GDP in 2026, 
before narrowing to 3.6% in 2027. Against this backdrop, we expect expenditure to 
become more selective, with priority given to strategic and advanced-stage projects while 
lower-priority spending is delayed or rationalized.
Risks remain sensitive to oil activities and geopolitics. Renewed regional escalation 
remains the main risk to the oil-led recovery we expect in 2027 through potential 
disruption to production and exports. An AI-downside scenario would weigh on non-oil 
investment, while heightened domestic security threats could further constrain non-oil 
investment and renew risks to oil exports.

101
Egypt: Resilience Prevails
Yomna Moheieldin 
(44 20) 7677 0771
Expect external buffers should be sufficient to absorb the still-elevated energy 
deficit, with FX continuing to act as the primary absorber of external pressure. 
With inflation returning to a downward trend and EM sentiment remaining 
supportive, we see the CBE resuming its easing cycle in 2Q27.
External resilience, but risks rise with the duration of the shock. Egypt has shown its 
ability to withstand the external shock in 2026. Our base case of oil prices in the US$70-
95/bbl range in FY27 and FY28 remains manageable, with the current account deficit at 
4.9% of GDP in FY27 and 4.4% in FY28, supported by strong remittance inflows and a 
gradual recovery in Suez Canal receipts. However, sustained oil prices above US$100/bbl 
would widen the current account deficit to 5.7% of GDP or more, putting greater pressure 
on external buffers. October's temporary deterioration in EM sentiment showed how 
quickly Egypt's buffers can be put to the test.  In our 2027 base case, supportive EM 
sentiment, strong remittances, and adequate external buffers allow Egypt to absorb the 
still-elevated energy deficit, with FX continuing to act as the primary shock absorber.
Inflation outlook remains highly sensitive to the FX path. We expect inflation to remain 
on a decelerating trend through 1H27, before accelerating temporarily in 3Q27 on 
unfavorable base effects. Inflation should then resume its downward trend, ending 2027 
at 11.9%Y and averaging 11.5%Y for the year, before declining further through 2028 to 
average 9.5%Y in 4Q28. Our outlook incorporates further fiscal consolidation measures, 
but remains particularly sensitive to the FX path.
Cautious easing to resume with a delay. The CBE delivered a surprise rate hike in 
October amid an episode of FX pressure. However, we expect it to remain on hold in the 
near term, before resuming its easing cycle in 2Q27. With inflation returning to a 
downward trend and emerging market sentiment remaining supportive (in our base case), 
we see limited rationale for maintaining higher real rates. As inflation approaches single 
digits in May, we expect the CBE to resume cutting, delivering a cumulative 400bp of cuts 
in 2027 and a further 300bp in 2028.
Growth remains strong, supported by domestic demand. We expect Egypt's growth to 
remain robust at 5.2%Y in FY27 and 4.9%Y in FY28, following 5.1%Y growth in FY26. 
Private consumption remains the primary growth engine, alongside some support from 
gross investment. 
Risks remain high. Persistently high oil prices could slow disinflation and delay the 
resumption of CBE easing to 2H27. A prolonged deterioration in EM sentiment, from an AI-
downside scenario or renewed regional escalation, poses a more significant risk through 
portfolio outflows and renewed EGP pressure. In that scenario, we would expect the CBE 
to hike by 200bp in 2027 rather than deliver the cuts in our base case.

102
Turkey: Worth the Wait  
Georgi Deyanov
(44 20) 7425 7006
We see subdued domestic demand recovering from mid-2027 onwards, helping 
GDP growth accelerate gradually. We expect disinflation to gain pace in 1H27, 
but the CBT to remain cautious and ease to 27.5% in 2027 and 24% in 2028.
GDP growth to recover from mid-2027 onward. We expect GDP growth to improve to 
4.1%Y in 2027 and 4.6%Y in 2028. On the domestic side, we expect private consumption 
growth to remain subdued in 2H26 and 1H27 but to recover as the negative impact from 
higher energy prices on real disposable income dissipates and domestic monetary 
conditions ease. Similarly, we see fixed investment growth recovering more visibly in 2H27 
and 2028. On the external side, we expect export growth to accelerate already in early 
2027, thanks to easing geopolitical tensions and robust global demand. That said, imports 
growth should also accelerate, but by less than exports, resulting in net exports 
contribution to GDP growth turning positive in 2027 and 2028.
Disinflation to gain pace in 1H27. Amid subdued private consumption growth, we expect 
the disinflation process to remain in place and even accelerate from March 2027 onwards. 
We see inflation easing to around 23%Y at end-2027, helped by relatively high real 
interest rates and balanced consumer demand. Yet, we expect underlying disinflation to 
slow down in 2028 as domestic demand growth recovers, and see year-end inflation 
falling to 18%Y. Regulatory price decisions, as well as the minimum wage increase in 2028, 
remain major uncertainties for the inflation outlook.
CBT likely to maintain a cautious easing cycle. We still expect the CBT to lower its key 
policy rate by 100bp to 35% at its upcoming December meeting this year. Further ahead, 
we see it maintaining a cautious easing cycle of 100bp steps at each meeting in 2027 until 
December, when we expect it to slow the pace of easing to 50bp and maintain that pace 
into 2028. Near-term risks to our forecast remain skewed to the upside, as major central 
banks are most likely to continue tightening monetary policy into 1Q27. In the more 
medium term, a potential increase in the fiscal policy uncertainty after the presidential 
and parliamentary elections in early 2028 could restrain the CBT from easing at a faster 
pace.
Risks. In case of significant further tightening of global financial conditions and 
subsequent stagnation in global growth, we see the CBT initially tightening monetary 
policy to 40% in 2027 before easing to 25% in 2028. Should global demand and inflation 
prove stronger than in our base case, we would expect the CBT to ease monetary policy at 
a much slower pace. If AI investment disappoints against expectations, we see scope for 
the CBT to deliver a faster and shallower easing cycle in 2027 and 2028. Finally, an AI-led 
productivity boost would likely result in a slightly deeper easing cycle than in our base 
case.

103
Israel: Holding Through Cross-Currents
Georgi Deyanov
(44 20) 7425 7006
Despite domestic and external political uncertainty, we see GDP growth 
remaining robust. Near-term subdued inflation pressures are likely to dissipate 
in mid-2027 on the back of recent FX weakness. With inflation rising and 
stabilising around the midpoint of the tolerance band, we see the BOI 
remaining on hold through the forecast horizon.
Robust growth despite elevated uncertainty. We expect GDP growth to remain driven by 
robust fixed investment and private consumption. Yet, as the economy catches up to its 
long-term trend, we see GDP growth easing to 4.3%Y in 2027 and 3.7%Y in 2028. We see 
fixed investment growth remaining well supported by rising ICT and defense expenditures, 
but also expect construction activity to slow due to declining residential property prices. 
Private consumption growth should remain robust over the forecast horizon, backed by 
robust real income growth amid a tight labor market. On the external side, we expect the 
net exports contribution to turn slightly positive in 2027 and continue improving in 2028 
on the back of robust external demand for Israeli exports.  
Subdued inflation to rise from 2H27 onwards. While we see headline inflation remaining 
below the 2%Y midpoint of the target range in the near term, we think it will accelerate 
gradually to 2%Y by end-2027 on the back of higher food and core inflation. Further 
ahead, we see headline inflation rising temporarily slightly above 2%Y in 2028, with core 
inflation supported by robust real income growth and supply constraints, as well as the 
recent depreciation of the ILS against major currencies. We see risks to the inflation 
outlook skewed to the upside in the event that higher defense spending leads to tax 
increases.
The BOI to remain steady amid elevated uncertainty. Having lowered its key policy rate 
close to 3.25%, we now see the BOI remaining on hold throughout our forecast horizon, 
against our previous expectation for it to ease to 3%. The prospect of inflation 
accelerating in 2H27 on the back of recent FX depreciation, coupled with inflation 
expectations hovering around the 2%Y midpoint of the target range, is likely to allow the 
central bank to look through near-term inflation weakness. With core inflation 
accelerating to around 2%Y in 3Q27, we expect the policy rate being close to the 3% 
neutral level will allow it to stabilize around that level over 2028. We see uncertainty 
about the policy rate outlook closely linked to geopolitical tensions and FX performance.
Risks. In case of a visible AI productivity boost, we see higher GDP growth but still 
moderate inflation keeping the BOI policy rate close to the 3% neutral level. If AI 
investment falls short of expectations, we see the BOI easing the key policy rate – with a 
delay due to FX depreciation risks in 4Q27 and 2028 – to a below-neutral level. If global 
demand and inflation prove stronger, it could tighten to 4.25% by the end of 2028. If 
global financial conditions tighten materially but subsequent recession risks emerge, the 
BOI could tighten initially to 3.75% by mid-2027 but then ease to 2.75% by end-2028.

Czech Republic: Near the Limit
Georgi Deyanov
(44 20) 7425 7006
We expect GDP to recover only gradually, supported by improving external 
demand. While we see inflation peaking around 3%Y in 1Q27, we do not expect 
the CNB to tighten further that quarter. Further ahead, we see the central bank 
easing to 3.75% in 4Q27 and 3.50% in 1Q28.
GDP growth to recover only gradually. We expect GDP growth to improve slightly to 
1.9%Y in 2027 and 2.3%Y in 2028 on the back of improving external demand. Meanwhile, 
we see private consumption growth moderating in 2027 before recovering in 2028, due to 
the negative impact of higher energy prices on households' real disposable income and 
tighter monetary policy. Despite the planned increase in public investment, we see 
investment growth moderating as a result of the challenging environment for the autos 
industry. We see net exports continuing to contribute negatively to GDP growth in 2027 
before turning positive in 2028.
Low productivity gains to keep core inflation elevated. We see both headline and core 
inflation remaining elevated until 1Q27 as the energy shock continues to pass through to 
transportation services and household energy prices. With these effects starting to 
dissipate, we expect headline inflation to moderate to around 2.5%Y in 2Q27 and converge 
towards the 2%Y target by year-end. That said, we project inflation to rise again slightly 
above the target in early 2028 due to higher food and sticky core inflation. We see 
elevated wage growth and low labor productivity gains sustaining underlying inflation 
pressures, despite the moderation in imputed rents inflation, keeping inflation in the upper 
range of the tolerance band over the forecast horizon.
CNB to be reluctant to hike further. We expect the CNB to keep its policy rate 
unchanged at 4% despite risks of inflation temporarily approaching the 3%Y upper limit of 
the tolerance band in 1Q27. That said, further acceleration in core inflation to around 
3.5%Y could trigger an additional 25bp hike in that quarter, we believe. Further ahead, 
with inflation easing closer to the 2%Y target in 2H27, we see the CNB lowering its key 
policy rate to 3.75% in 4Q27 and 3.50% in 1Q28. Sticky and elevated wage growth, 
combined with low labor productivity gains, is likely to prevent the central bank from 
easing below its estimate of the neutral policy rate at 3%.
Risks. A demand-driven pick-up in external growth and inflation could prompt the CNB to 
tighten further to 4.50% in 2027 and remain on hold in 2028. Similarly, a continued rise in 
global bond yields could translate into further tightening in 2027 but a reversal in 2028. 
On the downside, AI investment falling short of expectations would help the CNB reverse 
the 2026 monetary policy tightening in 2027 and ease further below the 3% neutral rate 
in 2028. A more pronounced AI productivity boost could help the bank lower its key 
policy rate more than in our base case in 2028.

Poland: Measured Moderation
Georgi Deyanov
(44 20) 7425 7006
We see GDP growth softening to 2.9%Y in 2027 as the RRF-led investment 
boost fades. Despite high and volatile headline inflation, we believe the NBP 
will remain on hold until 3Q27 as core inflation remains stable. The upcoming 
general elections create higher-than-usual uncertainty.
Softer GDP growth ahead. We expect GDP growth to ease to 2.9%Y in 2027 and remain 
relatively moderate at 2.7%Y in 2028. Despite the negative effects of higher energy 
inflation on household real income, we see EU funds absorption and defense spending 
supporting fixed investment growth through 1H27. Further ahead, as the positive effects 
of the EU's Recovery and Resilience Facility (RRF) fade away, we believe private 
consumption will take over as the main driver of GDP growth in 2H27 and 2028, 
supported by lower effective personal income tax rates through the increase in non-
taxable income thresholds. On the external side, we see the net exports contribution 
remaining roughly neutral in 2027 but improve slightly in 2028 as import-intensive fixed 
investment growth moderates.  
Volatile headline amidst broadly stable core inflation. We expect headline inflation to 
remain close to the 3.5%Y upper bound of the NBP's tolerance band for the rest of 2026, 
suppressed by the government's fuel tax cuts. Yet, as the government's program expires, 
we see headline inflation rising temporarily to about  4%Y in January and February before 
easing to around 3.5%Y in March and 3%Y in April. Further ahead, we see headline 
inflation remaining volatile on the back of base effects, but expect it overall to converge 
to the 2.5%Y inflation target in 4Q27 and 1H28. Balanced wage growth, coupled with 
robust labor productivity growth, should keep unit labor costs (ULC) and core inflation 
dynamics close to the 2.5%Y target in 2027 and 2028. Uncertainty about next year's 
inflation outlook remains highly dependent on potential government decisions to extend 
the fuel tax cuts into 1Q27 and cap electricity prices on top of gas prices.
NBP to remain vigilant. We continue to expect the NBP to leave its key policy rate 
unchanged at 3.75% until 3Q27, when we see scope for a 25bp rate cut on the back of 
moderating core and stabilising headline inflation. Meanwhile, we expect the central bank 
to continue signalling readiness to tighten monetary policy, should regulated energy 
inflation rise in early 2027. Further ahead, we see scope for it to ease to 3.25% in 2028 on 
the back of inflation stabilising slightly below the target. Uncertainty around our forecast 
is a function of FX performance in 2027, especially going into the October general 
elections.
Risks. Stronger external demand could push the NBP to tighten to 4.50%, while subdued 
AI investment would allow it to ease to 3% in 2027 and 2.50% in 2028. Higher energy and 
global bond yields would require tightening to 4.25% in 2027, followed by easing to 3.25% 
in 2028 as the economy stagnates. Finally, a visible AI productivity boost would allow for 
higher growth and moderate inflation, resulting in delayed easing to 3% in 2028.

Hungary: Convergence Reawakened
Georgi Deyanov
(44 20) 7425 7006
We expect GDP growth to embark on an acceleration path from 2Q27, while 
real exchange rate appreciation helps inflation converge to the new, lower 
2.5%Y inflation target in 2028. We see the NBH continuing to normalize 
monetary policy cautiously, with an eye on inflation expectations. 
Growth on an acceleration path. We see Hungarian GDP growth accelerating to 2.9%Y in 
2027 and 3.5%Y in 2028, backed by a pick-up in fixed investment growth as both RRF and 
Cohesion funds absorption improves. Meanwhile, we expect private consumption growth 
to remain robust thanks to consumer-friendly fiscal consolidation and accelerated real 
income convergence in sight of the euro adoption path. Despite expanding export capacity, 
we see accelerating investment growth keeping the net exports contribution to growth 
broadly neutral over the forecast horizon.
Inflation to converge to 2.5%Y in 2028. We expect headline inflation to accelerate 
gradually over the coming months on the back of recovering food deflation, rising fuel 
inflation and higher excise duties on tobacco. Yet, we see headline inflation peaking 
around 3%Y in mid-2027 and gradually converging to the new, lower 2.5%Y inflation 
target by year-end. Further ahead,  real HUF exchange rate appreciation against the EUR 
should contribute to weaker retrospective services repricing and slightly negative core 
goods inflation in 2028. As such, we expect overall inflation dynamics to align with the 
2.5%Y inflation target in 2028. Upside risks to our inflation forecast depend on the 
outcome of negotiations over the pace of minimum wage hikes and potential 
normalization of subsidized household energy prices. 
An expectations-driven easing cycle by the NBH. We continue to expect the NBH to 
resume normalizing monetary policy by lowering its key policy rate by 25bp at its 
December, March and June core meetings, to 4.75%, before pausing in 2H27 as it strives to 
anchor inflation to the lower 2.5%Y target. Further ahead, we see scope for the central 
bank to lower its key policy rate to 4.25% in 2028, closer to our 4% estimate of the 
neutral nominal policy rate. Uncertainty around our policy rate forecast stems from the 
pace of recovery in inflation expectations from their current subdued level and FX 
dynamics amid the uncertain global environment.
Risks. Our four global risk scenarios pose challenges for the NBH in achieving the lower 
inflation target in 2028. Stronger external demand and inflation or a continued rise in 
global bond yields would likely keep the NBH on hold for longer. A significant AI-led 
productivity boost could prompt the NBH lower its key policy rate to 4% in 2028. Should 
AI investment disappoint against expectations, we would see the central bank easing to 
4% in 2027 and 3.50% in 2028.

South Africa: Reforms Lead, Growth Follows
Andrea Masia
(27 11) 587 0820
Reform progress translates into incrementally stronger rates of productivity 
growth over our forecast horizon, lifting potential output in the process. This 
supply driven improvement ultimately helps deliver lower levels of trend 
inflation, facilitating an unwind of the restrictive monetary policy stance. The 
near-term cyclical outlook faces challenges, however.
We expect South Africa's economic recovery to gather momentum over the coming 
two years, supported by a gradual improvement in potential growth and fiscal 
fundamentals. We forecast real GDP growth of 1.1% in 2026, accelerating to 1.6% in 2027 
and 2.0% in 2028.  In our baseline, underlying domestic demand remains subdued in 2027, 
with household consumption growth impacted by the  delayed effects of policy rate 
tightening and slowing credit growth. Fixed investment, on the other had, improves, led 
primarily by the public sector.  We expect a more durable recovery in 2028, when both 
consumption and investment growth accelerate.
We think South Africa can lock in productivity gains from better utilization of existing 
infrastructure and capital, particularly across its rail, road and port network. This dynamic 
represents the more likely near-term growth driver than an  AI related productivity shock, 
if our interactions with corporate management teams are anything to go by. We estimate 
that potential GDP growth will increase from approximately 1.4% in 2026 to 1.8% by 
2028. For potential growth to break above 2% or more, the country needs fixed 
investment beyond 15% of GDP, or alternatively, much faster levels of AI adoption. 
Indeed, reform trackers are showing signs of progress and we expect successful delivery 
to gradually lower logistics and energy costs, improve asset utilization and raise 
productive capacity. That said,  there are still binding constraints:  in electricity, the 
principal obstacle has migrated from generation capacity to transmission infrastructure, 
grid access and regulatory certainty. Freight logistics offer clearer evidence of progress, 
with Transnet rail volumes recovering from 149 million tonnes in FY22/23 to 168 million 
tonnes in FY25/26. Private-sector appetite for infrastructure investment is evident, but 
project bankability and implementation constraints remain. The Ministry of Public Works 
and Infrastructure has identified around 4% of GDP in implementable projects, yet only 
around one in six infrastructure tenders reaches award, per the Ministry of Public Works 
and Infrastructure. Municipal capacity, procurement execution and rehabilitation of 
existing networks are therefore more important than the availability of funding alone.
 South Africa's improving fiscal dynamics have been a key underpin to our macro view 
over the past year or so, with the 2026 Medium-Term Budget Policy Statement 
reinforcing our expectation that the debt ratio has peaked and will likely edge lower from 
here. We forecast budget deficits that oscillate around 3% of GDP over the Medium-Term 
Expenditure Framework, with embedded primary surpluses that exceed debt stabilizing 
primary balances.  The recent GDP re-basing, which increased the measured size of the

From a funding perspective, a new government bond buyback programme, together with 
reductions in weekly  bond supply, should help contain refinancing risk and reduce reliance 
on more expensive domestic issuance. These measures provide additional support for the 
cost of debt and sovereign creditworthiness and will result in an upgrade to the country's 
credit ratings, in our view. Municipal finances and contingent liabilities represent 
important risks to the sovereign balance sheet.
Cyclical dynamics look challenged, however, particularly in light of the unfolding 
energy shock. Our baseline projection for headline CPI peaks  in 4Q26 before moderating 
and reaching the SARB's 3% target in 2028. The trajectory is driven in large part by 
benchmark crude oil prices that remain around the US$90-100 mark through 1Q27 and  
wider-for-longer crack spreads. An El Niño-driven rebound in food prices through 2027 
also forms part of our baseline. 
Beyond the headline numbers, there are encouraging signs that core inflation pressures 
are being contained and that inflation momentum is rolling over. Our inflation shock 
momentum index is signalling momentum loss in goods and services, which we estimate 
will prevent core inflation from accelerating materially from here. Incremental progress 
on supply-side reform should provide a important anchor for core CPI, in our view.
We acknowledge that the SARB sees upside risks to inflation, and is concerned that 
inflation expectations are proving to be stickier than its models assumed. However, 
recent fuel levy subsidies, a decline in benchmark crude prices and a stronger FX are 
addressing some of those concerns and may drive a moderation in its headline CPI 
forecast over the coming months. Furthermore, if our ISMI framework is correct,  the 
SARB's core CPI forecast will also be lowered. We believe patience is preferred under such 
circumstances, with disinflation coming through a tighter for longer policy stance, not 
necessarily by additional tightening.

Brazil: Growth Weakens as Inflation Keeps Rate Relief on 
Hold
Ana Madeira
(55 11) 3048 9582
Thiago Machado
(55 11) 3048 6249
We expect a deeper growth slowdown in 2027, as stretched household balance 
sheets, fading labor market resilience and a smaller fiscal impulse weigh on 
domestic demand. Meanwhile, food, fuel and policy-related cost pressures 
should lift inflation, keeping it above target despite weaker activity. 
Heightened fiscal uncertainty and de-anchored inflation expectations should 
keep the BCB on hold through end-2027, before easing begins in 2028. Lower 
rates should support a mild recovery in consumption and investment, but 
monetary policy should remain restrictive. Delivering a sustained fiscal 
adjustment remains key to containing risks as public debt continues to rise.
A deeper slowdown in 2027 before a mild recovery in 2028. We revise down our 2027 
growth forecast, as we see mounting downside pressures entering next year. Private 
consumption should bear the brunt of the slowdown: higher inflation will likely erode real 
wage gains, while elevated indebtedness and record-high debt-servicing burdens 
increasingly constrain household spending. Consumer confidence has already fallen to its 
lowest level in four years, and we expect labor market resilience to fade as unemployment 
starts to rise in 2027. Meanwhile, a smaller fiscal impulse relative to 2026, as income- and 
credit-support measures fade, should add to the drag on consumption. Investment should 
also remain subdued, as still-high borrowing costs and mounting corporate financial 
distress limit companies' capacity to expand. Net exports should remain supportive, 
although less than previously expected as El Niño weighs on agricultural output. We 
expect growth momentum to recover mildly in 2028, led by private consumption and 
investment as declining interest rates support some improvement in credit conditions.
Supply and policy pressures to lift inflation, keeping above the 3%Y target. Inflation 
dynamics are deteriorating, so we increase our inflation forecast for 2027, reflecting El 
Niño-related food price pressures, the recently approved phaseout of the 6x1 work scale, 
higher fuel prices, and a higher-than-expected VAT tax rate. We expect El Niño to push 
food inflation higher, particularly in 1H27 led by fresh produce, while the reduction in 
weekly working hours, with wages unchanged, should raise labor costs and add to services 
inflation. Meanwhile, lower fuel subsidies and higher fuel taxes should translate into 
higher prices at the pump, with the new VAT rate adding further pressure. Slower growth 
should only partially offset these supply-side and policy-driven pressures. For 2028, we 
expect inflation to moderate as these effects gradually fade, but remaining above the 
target, while a smaller fiscal impulse should help contain demand-driven price pressures.
An extended pause for interest rates, with the next move still a cut. We maintain our 
call for the BCB to hold the policy rate at 13.75% through end-2027, as heightened fiscal 
uncertainty and its implications for inflation expectations should limit the scope for

further easing. Policymakers will likely remain in wait-and-see mode, we think, seeking 
signs of a credible fiscal adjustment from the reelected government. A fiscal plan that 
reduces uncertainty and helps partially re-anchor inflation expectations could create room 
for rate cuts to resume. With rates already substantially contractionary and growth set to 
weaken further, the bar for hikes remains high, in our view, and we think the BCB's next 
move will be a cut. We expect cuts to start in 2028 but end above neutral levels. The need 
to keep a still-contractionary monetary policy stance over the next two years would be 
driven by inflation still above the target on that horizon.
Fiscal uncertainty remains the key risk ahead. Investors will closely monitor whether the 
government signals a credible improvement in fiscal dynamics. Resilient growth over the 
past four years has helped keep primary deficits relatively contained, but we expect 
slower growth to weigh on revenues going forward. As a result, we expect the primary 
balance to fall below the lower bound of the fiscal target range in both 2027 and 2028. 
The nominal deficit should remain large, reflecting high interest costs, while rising 
mandatory spending limits budget flexibility. Together, these pressures should keep the 
gross debt-to-GDP ratio on an upward trajectory – reforms that moderate expenditure 
growth and improve fiscal credibility could support a more sustainable debt trajectory.
Our global scenarios imply limited upside but more challenging downside risks. Brazil's 
relatively closed economy and elevated domestic rates should limit gains from an 
aggregate demand upside, with modestly stronger growth and higher inflation delaying 
BCB easing. AI productivity upside would offer limited near-term support, but gradual 
efficiency gains could lift growth, lower inflation and allow rates to fall modestly below 
baseline over time. Conversely, an AI investment downside scenario would weaken 
exports, consumption and investment, with softer demand eventually supporting faster 
disinflation and monetary easing. A rates and oil downside scenario would pose a more 
persistent inflation challenge, as higher energy costs squeeze domestic spending despite 
some support from oil revenues. We would expect the BCB to initially hike, before weaker 
activity prompts cuts in 2028, leaving rates above baseline.

Mexico: Cyclical Recovery on a Fiscal Tightrope
Fernando  Sedano 
(55 11) 3048 6605
Nicolas Eterovic
(1 212) 761 1245
Julia Lobato
(55 11) 3048 6016
Easing trade uncertainty should support a recovery in investment and growth 
through 2027-28. Resilient US demand – including for IT and AI-related exports 
– helps, but  spillovers remain limited. Keeping investment grade requires fiscal 
discipline, increasing the importance of PPPs for infrastructure. Inflation stays 
above target as services pressures persist and currency and weather risks 
complicate disinflation. We expect a final Banxico cut in March 2027.
Investment should sustain a gradual cyclical recovery. We expect growth of 1.9% in 
2027 and 2.1% in 2028, from 1.7% in 2026. We expect USMCA uncertainty, which has 
weighed on private investment since 2H24, to ease gradually, potentially through a 
bilateral US agreement. This should allow selected expansion projects to resume 
alongside maintenance and replacement spending. Public capex has led the recovery from 
a low base, while private investment remains uneven. PPPs should broaden the 
infrastructure pipeline, but implementation takes time and a sustained private capex cycle 
requires stronger confidence and regulatory clarity. Fiscal pressures limit public spending's 
ability to offset private-sector weakness.
US demand supports exports, with a growing role for IT. Resilient US activity should 
underpin manufacturing exports, including IT hardware and AI related shipments. However, 
high imported content and more capital-intensive production imply less domestic value-
added and smaller employment spillovers than in autos. We do not assume a meaningful 
domestic AI-driven productivity gain. Consumption should remain relatively resilient, 
supported by real incomes and government transfers, but subdued employment 
momentum limits the acceleration. Investment and export gains should reach households 
gradually, with  modest support from remittances.
Services persistence limits progress toward the inflation target. We expect headline 
inflation to end 2027 at 3.8% and 2028 at 3.7%, remaining in the upper part of the 
tolerance range. Fading effects from earlier tax, tariff and energy pressures should provide 
some relief, while goods inflation has remained relatively contained and evidence of broad 
second-round effects is limited. Housing and other services remain persistent despite 
domestic slack, suggesting that the negative output gap alone will be insufficient to 
deliver convergence to 3%. As activity recovers, disinflation from weak demand should 
diminish, leaving services as the main constraint on convergence.
Currency pressures could complicate disinflation. Historically low FX pass-through 
could cushion the effect of peso depreciation, but poses upside  risks to core goods. A 
larger or more persistent adjustment could lift  import costs, with firms passing through 
those as demand improves. El Niño adds some food-price risk through agri disruptions, 
while fuel-tax adjustments can cushion energy shocks, subject to fiscal constraints.

112
Limited further easing before a prolonged hold. Banxico’s November 25bp cut to 6.25%  
renewed guidance for a prolonged pause. The majority emphasized persistent slack and 
lingering monetary restraint, judging that weak demand should also limit FX pass-through. 
Although the restored pause guidance argues against consecutive cuts, we think the bar 
for another targeted reduction is lower than communicated if the external backdrop 
improves. We now expect a final 25bp cut in March 2027, taking the rate to 6.0%, 
conditional on further disinflation, contained expectations and orderly FX developments. 
Fed policy remains a key constraint, while renewed peso weakness or persistent inflation 
could delay the move. Beyond this final cut, persistent services inflation, above-target 
expectations and a gradual activity recovery should favor a prolonged hold through 2027-
28. Further cuts would require more convincing disinflation, even if the Fed were to ease.
Most of the fiscal adjustment is behind us. The 2027 budget's more realistic 
assumptions support fiscal credibility, as preserving investment grade gains prominence in 
the policy debate. Still, we expect only a modest, gradual improvement in fiscal balances. 
Payroll, pensions and protected social transfers absorb a growing share of expenditure, 
while  Pemex support remains a recurring pressure. Physical investment and non-payroll 
operations carried most of the earlier adjustment, making further cuts increasingly costly 
for infrastructure and public services. Revenue execution is central, notably whether 
corporate income-tax measures and efforts to reduce evasion deliver the expected 
proceeds. A broader tax reform to strengthen recurring non-oil revenues will likely be 
needed to reconcile consolidation with investment and social-spending priorities and help 
preserve the investment grade status, although we see limited political appetite before 
the 2027 mid-term elections. The electoral cycle should support infrastructure spending 
at the margin, but fiscal constraints limit the overall growth impulse.
External developments drive alternative scenarios. Stronger US demand and clearer 
trade rules would lift exports, investment and later consumption; firmer inflation would 
limit easing and later warrant hikes. A US productivity acceleration would offer a smaller, 
export-led growth dividend and relief via import prices. A reversal in US AI investment 
would weaken US demand, cutting exports and private capex before reaching employment 
and consumption, eventually allowing more easing. An oil and rates shock would pose a 
harder trade-off: higher costs and peso depreciation might initially require tightening 
despite weaker activity, with relief only after inflation pressures subside.

Argentina: A Gradual Recovery Ahead of Elections
Fernando  Sedano 
(55 11) 3048 6605
Julia Lobato 
(55 11) 3048 6016
Growth remains export-led, with investment and consumption recovering 
gradually. The 2027 presidential election is the main uncertainty. We  expect 
orthodox policies, anchored by fiscal discipline and support for private 
investment. Stronger FX buffers should help contain electoral volatility.
Investment and exports should lead the gradual recovery. We expect growth of 2.4% in 
2027 and 3.0% in 2028, from 1.3% in 2026. Agriculture, additional oil pipeline capacity and 
RIGI projects should support exports, capex and construction, with mining initially 
contributing through project development. Electoral uncertainty could delay expansion 
decisions, while capital intensity limits immediate employment spillovers. Lower inflation 
should restore purchasing power, but household delinquency keeps consumer lending 
cautious, with commercial and USD lending recovering first. Improvements in real 
incomes, credit and employment are key to policy support.
Disinflation and monetary normalization remain gradual. We expect year-end inflation 
at 21.7% in 2027 and 17.1% in 2028. Softer core inflation and fiscal discipline support 
progress, but inertia and administered-price adjustments remain hurdles. Contractual 
utility-tariff increases should continue through 2027, without an election-year slowdown. 
The BCRA's focus on private transactional M2 makes recovering peso demand central to 
remonetization. Stronger activity and disinflation should allow reserve purchases to 
supply liquidity with less sterilization, while monetary expansion remains cautious. 
Nominal rates should decline overall, with more sustained normalization in 2028.
Reserve accumulation remains essential. Rising energy exports, agricultural receipts and 
investment inflows should support FX purchases, alongside a small current-account 
surplus. Treasury and BCRA FX debt payments absorb dollars, while dollar demand 
should rise ahead of elections. Multilateral financing and contingent credit lines offer 
options. Reducing upcoming FX financing needs and strengthening usable reserves would 
help limit volatility.
Fiscal equilibrium as non-negotiable. We expect primary surpluses and broadly balanced 
overall accounts, with little electoral fiscal stimulus. Revenue surprises are more likely to 
finance tax cuts than spending. Health, education, pensions and provincial transfers create 
pressures, while earlier cuts leave little room for more. Tax relief remains constrained by 
balanced accounts, as revenue costs precede benefits from investment and formalization.
Policy dominates the risks. Our rates and oil downside scenario combines tighter global 
financing with a post-election shift away from the current framework, lifting inflation and 
rates and possibly triggering recession. Our AI downside pairs weaker external conditions 
with limited policy changes that preserve the core framework, weakening growth slightly. 
Stronger global demand would support investment and credit, while AI-related spillovers 
remain modest. Policy credibility and reform durability outweigh US growth.

Chile: The Reform Dividend Takes Shape
Nicolas Eterovic
(1 212) 761 1245
Julia Lobato 
(55 11) 3048 6016
We expect Chile's economy to grow,  led by an investment recovery that should 
gradually spread into household demand. A year ago, we argued that higher 
copper prices and a shift toward pro-growth policies would support the next 
phase of the cycle. While the policy framework has improved, mining 
disruptions, higher energy costs and project delays have pushed the growth 
dividend into 2027. 
Chile's weak 2026 performance reflects delayed transmission rather than weak 
investment intentions. Higher copper prices supported exports and fiscal revenues, but 
supply constraints limited mining output and higher energy costs squeezed purchasing 
power. We forecast fixed investment growth of 7.9% in 2027 after a 0.5% contraction in 
2026, as construction advances. Our input-output analysis shows construction supports 
2.4 times as many jobs as an equivalent mining-demand shock, but labor costs and weak 
credit should limit private consumption growth to 1.5% in 2027.
Headline inflation should fall from 4.7% year on year at end-2026 to 2.3% by end-
2027, averaging 3.3% in 2027, with disinflation accelerating from 2Q27 as energy 
pressures fade. Core inflation should peak near 3.8% in 2Q27, average 3.6% and end 2027 
at 3.3%. Weak consumption and an investment-led recovery should contain demand 
pressures, while labor costs, services inflation and exchange-rate pass-through are risks.
We expect the BCCh to hold its policy rate at 4.50% throughout 2027, resuming easing 
in 2028. October's hawkish shift reflected external inflation risks and tighter global 
financial conditions. Upside inflation surprises could trigger hikes, which remain outside 
our baseline, while faster core convergence and well anchored expectations could bring 
cuts forward. Renewed external shocks could prolong the pause.
Investment execution remains the main growth upside risk. A larger pipeline, 
predictable permitting and improved incentives could accelerate construction and 
household-income gains. Consolidation has relied on mining revenues and restrained 
capital spending. The proposed 2027 budget targets structural and effective deficits of 
1.8% of GDP. Making room for public works while containing recurrent spending will be 
demanding. Reforms support potential growth, but benefits require productivity gains, 
labor-market adjustments and execution.
Chile's exposure to our four global scenarios is asymmetric. Under our aggregate 
demand and AI productivity upside scenarios, 2027 growth stays near our 3.5% baseline, 
with year-end rates at 5.50% and 4.50%, respectively. An AI downside scenario would 
modestly reduce growth and bring rates to 4.25%. A rates and oil downside would lower 
growth to 3.2% in 2027 and 1.8% in 2028, while inflation would push 2027 rates to 5.50%. 
We expect the current-account deficit to widen from 0.6% of GDP in 2026 to 3.2% in 
2027. Investment execution, employment and inflation expectations remain key signposts.

Colombia: A Delicate Rebalacing Act
Nicolas Eterovic
(1 212) 761 1245
Julia Lobato 
(55 11) 3048 6016
Colombia will enter 2027 with the economy facing a gradual rebalancing rather 
than a sharp adjustment. We expect real GDP growth to slow  before recovering  
in 2028. The moderation reflects weaker private consumption, slower 
government spending growth and still-restrictive financial conditions. Headline 
inflation should decline, allowing BanRep to begin a gradual normalization of 
monetary policy. 
Colombia's challenge is to slow domestic demand enough to reduce inflation without 
undermining recovery. The final domestic demand contribution to growth should fall 
from 4.2pp in 2026 to 2.0pp in 2027, recovering to 3.6pp in 2028. In 2027, private 
consumption growth slows from 2.2% in 2026 to 1.3%, government spending from 10.7% 
to 2.8%, and fixed investment from 4.0% to 3.1%, before accelerating to 5.0% in 2028 as 
financing improves.
This implies a controlled slowdown, with investment supporting a more balanced 
recovery in 2028. The uncertainty is whether investment can offset weaker consumption 
and public spending quickly enough.
Fiscal consolidation is central to this adjustment. We expect a primary fiscal adjustment 
of around 1.5pp of GDP in 2027 and 3.0pp in 2028, versus Hacienda's 2.39pp and 4.29pp. 
Primary deficits narrow to 3.0% and 1.6% of GDP, and overall deficits to 7.9% and 6.5%, 
assuming stable interest costs. Net debt reaches 65.2% and 65.9% of GDP, above 
Hacienda's 64.4% and 63.7%. Credible consolidation would restrain demand and create 
room for monetary easing.
Inflation should gradually decline. We expect headline inflation to decline from 6.6% at 
end-2026 to 5.1% at end-2027 and 4.3% at end-2028; core should fall from 6.0% to 4.5% 
and 3.8%. Persistent above-target core inflation limits aggressive easing.  On the monetary 
side, we expect BanRep to hold its policy rate at 12.25% from end-2026 through 1Q27, 
then cut to 11.75% in 2Q27, 11.25% in 3Q27 and 10.5% by year-end, reaching 8.5% at end-
2028. This represents normalization, with policy remaining restrictive.
Across our scenarios, 2027 growth remains at 1.4-1.5%, widening to 1.7-3.3% in 2028. 
Year-end inflation ranges are 5.0-5.2% and 4.3-4.5%, respectively. An aggregate demand 
upside scenario would be most inflationary, keeping year-end rates at 11.0% in 2027 and 
9.5% in 2028; AI productivity upside would support stronger growth through supply gains, 
with rates at 10.5% and 8.5%; AI downside would weaken investment and demand, 
allowing faster easing to 10.25% and 8.0%. A rates and oil downside scenario would 
deliver the weakest growth and higher near-term rates, falling to 8.25% by end-2028 as 
weaker demand offsets the inflation shock.

Peru: A Chance to Broaden the Recovery
Nicolas Eterovic
(1 212) 761 1245
Julia Lobato
(55 11) 3048 6016
We expect real GDP growth to edge up from 2.9% in 2026 to 3.0% in 2027, led 
by fixed investment as domestic demand moderates. El Niño, elevated inflation 
and limited execution capacity will keep the recovery uneven, but the 
investment pipeline could support stronger growth beyond 2027.
Peru will enter 2027 with stronger domestic-demand momentum than headline GDP 
suggests. Capital-goods imports rose roughly 20%, domestic cement consumption 10%, 
vehicle sales 45%, consumer-goods imports 27% and internal VAT collection 7%. Vehicle 
sales approach 300,000 units annually. Momentum should support year-end activity 
before tighter global financing and higher energy prices moderate demand.
We forecast GDP growth of 3.0% in 2027, up from 2.9% in 2026. Fixed investment 
remains the driver, growing 7.8% after 12.1% in 2026, supported by mining and transport 
infrastructure. Private consumption slows from 3.6% to 2.7%. Imports remain strong, 
while exports offer little support.
El Niño will make the 2027 growth profile uneven.  Fishing conditions threaten another 
weak anchovy season, while agro-exporters have adjusted planting and harvesting, but 
employment effects are emerging. Construction, commerce and services remain resilient 
as primary sectors weaken ahead of climate normalization and reconstruction.
Mining investment could reach US$8bn in 2027, but we estimate that only 17% of 2026-
30 flows will materially expand output, versus 60% in 2011-15. The main new project, Tía 
María, is nearly 50% complete and could add 120,000 tonnes of copper. Permitting, illegal 
mining and weak exploration remain constraints.
We forecast average headline inflation falling from 3.8% in 2026 to 1.8% in 2027 as 
energy prices fall, climate conditions normalize and base effects fade. However, see 
core inflation rising from 2.6% to 3.3%. We therefore expect the policy rate to rise from 
4.75% at end-2026 to 5.0% in 2027 before easing toward 4.25% in 2028.
Political risks to macroeconomic stability have diminished, but reforms should remain 
gradual and negotiated. The lack of a congressional majority, limited state capacity and 
security concerns constrain implementation and confidence. Investment and favorable 
terms of trade should sustain growth near 3%. 
Across our scenarios, we envisage a broadly balanced outlook, but with different 
policy implications. Stronger aggregate demand or AI-led productivity gains lift 2027 
growth toward 3.3%, although the latter allows rates to normalize faster. An AI 
investment correction would leave growth near 3.2% and keep policy closer to baseline, 
while the combination of higher oil prices and a global rates sell-off is the main downside 
risk, reducing growth toward 2.9% and requiring rates to rise toward 5.5%.
